<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **r-zero-modes** [claim](#stmt-lem-zero-modes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-zero-modes): Divisibility by the cubed pair bracket, unique factorization and a monomial basis count -> polynomial form of zero modes and their dimension
- **r-unperturbed** [claim](#stmt-thm-unperturbed) / [proof](#pm-h-11): Recalled from the companion manuscript -> uniform Fock-space coercivity and filled-sector gap
- **r-frame** [claim](#stmt-locality-frame) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-frame): Binomial tail and difference bounds, summation by parts, coherent resolution -> rapid decay of frame overlaps
- **r-common** [claim](#stmt-locality-common-frames) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-common-frames): Reuse windows at the larger flux and restrict indices -> shared slots across nearby fluxes
- **r-shells** [claim](#stmt-locality-core-shells) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-core-shells): Telescoping near-best approximants at dyadic radii -> shell decomposition and moment bounds
- **r-calculus** [claim](#stmt-locality-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-calculus): Product estimate, disjoint-ball commutators and weighted sums -> closure of rapid locality under products and commutators
- **r-toeplitz** [claim](#stmt-locality-toeplitz) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-toeplitz): Coherent resolution of the Toeplitz lift and rotation resolution of the Hamiltonian -> physical rapid interactions for the Hamiltonian and the perturbation
- **r-dynamics** [claim](#stmt-locality-dynamics) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-dynamics): Truncation, iterated commutators and Duhamel restoration -> polynomial-in-time propagation of locality
- **r-filters** [claim](#stmt-locality-filters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-filters): Integrate the propagation bound against the filter -> locality of time-filtered operators
- **r-difference** [claim](#stmt-locality-difference) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-difference): Duhamel formula with radius bookkeeping -> weighted evolution difference bounds
- **r-compression** [claim](#stmt-locality-functional-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-functional-calculus): Rotate two slot copies, take vacuum expectation, then Fourier functional calculus -> locality of compression and smooth functions
- **r-rowsym** [claim](#stmt-locality-row-symbols) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-row-symbols): Gamma-function derivative bounds and Fourier wavepackets over windows -> local pair row squares
- **r-onebody** [claim](#stmt-locality-one-body-symbol) / [proof](#proof-stmt-locality-one-body-symbol): Slot matrix elements by summation by parts, then vacuum compression -> local interaction for diagonal symbols
- **r-pinning-gap** [claim](#stmt-pinning-uniform-gap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-uniform-gap): Compare columns of annihilators after rounding the weights, then remove the rounding -> uniform gap along the pinning path
- **r-pinning-transport** [claim](#stmt-pinning-generator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-generator): Spectral filter of an affine-subtracted commutator source -> local unitary path transporting zero spaces
- **r-northern** [claim](#stmt-pinning-northern-observable) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-northern-observable): Apply the difference bound to the generator and take partial-trace shells -> northern observable change bounds
- **r-weighted-transport** [claim](#stmt-pinning-weighted-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-weighted-transport): Integrate the commutator with the generator along the path using annular pieces -> interaction for the weighted observable change
- **r-one-sided** [claim](#stmt-pinning-one-sided) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-one-sided): Raising-operator filter, local proportionality of convex and concave parts, negative square identity -> one-sided bound on the compressed change
- **r-calibration-cost** [claim](#stmt-pinning-calibration-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pinning-calibration-cost): Differentiate the expectation along the growth path and use commutator calculus -> cost of changing calibration state
- **r-branch** [claim](#stmt-lem-flags-branch-algebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-flags-branch-algebra): Divisibility characterization and surjectivity by monomial symmetric functions -> orthogonal branch decomposition
- **r-polar** [claim](#stmt-lem-flags-polar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-flags-polar): Restricted gap on the south-empty sector, emptying-energy identity, filtered contraction and smooth polar map -> occupation gap and local polar representatives
- **r-weighted-step** [claim](#stmt-prop-flags-weighted-step) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-flags-weighted-step): Subtract the affine part and commute the remainder with local representatives -> block form of the weighted observable
- **r-northern-step** [claim](#stmt-lem-flags-northern-step) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-flags-northern-step): Northern observable corollary for the flag branch, commutation through the polar map for the electron branch -> northern branch comparison
- **r-reference-compat** [claim](#stmt-lem-flags-reference-compatibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-flags-reference-compatibility): Normal-ordered monomial expansion with identical frame entries -> agreement of reference expectations
- **r-readout** [claim](#stmt-thm-flags-local-readout) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-flags-local-readout): Descend the branching tree and close a bootstrap inequality for the best constant -> history-weighted bound on local observables
- **r-one-hole** [claim](#stmt-cor-flags-one-hole) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-flags-one-hole): Irreducibility, trace of the weight and Schur rotation averaging -> area-independent one-hole comparison
- **r-scalars** [claim](#stmt-lem-charge-scalars) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-charge-scalars): Exact scalar formula, positive second-difference form and continuous comparison -> scalar telescoping estimates
- **r-sums** [claim](#stmt-lem-charge-sums) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-charge-sums): Split the flux range at the weight scale and integrate the size function -> uniformly small error sums
- **r-charge-calibration** [claim](#stmt-lem-charge-calibration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-charge-calibration): Filled step at the nearest lower flux plus calibration cost comparisons -> calibrated electron step bound
- **r-pooled-first** [claim](#stmt-prop-charge-pooled) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-charge-calibration-2): Recursive branch comparison, one-hole treatment and sharing of electron scalars among flags for the first exponent -> pooled charge bound for the first exponent
- **r-weak-average** [claim](#stmt-cor-charge-weak-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-charge-weak-average): Rotation averaging of the pooled bound over the group -> averaged comparison with sublinear flux power
- **r-pooled-second** [claim](#stmt-prop-charge-pooled) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-charge-weak-average-2): Dyadic annuli, averaged bound at moderate flux, fixed-axis history bound at large flux -> pooled charge bound for the summable exponent
- **r-local-linear** [claim](#stmt-thm-charge-local-linear) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-charge-local-linear): Summable weight makes rotation averaging area independent -> linear dependence on the electron deficit
- **r-evacuation** [claim](#stmt-lem-evacuation-rows) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-evacuation-rows): Taylor expansion of the cutoff product, Gaussian pair moments and exponential tails -> pair energy after local loss
- **r-pair-spin-tail** [claim](#stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail): Multiplicity-freeness, oscillator limit of coupling coefficients and highest-vector resolution -> vanishing weight on fixed deficits and lifted block bound
- **r-square** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-square) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-square): Normal ordering with zero, one or two contractions -> decomposition of the square of the Hamiltonian
- **r-pair-coefficients** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-pair) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-pair): Iterated lowering and binomial expansion -> explicit pair coefficients and limits
- **r-three-body-gram** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-q) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-q): Antisymmetrization, swap compressions and highest-vector ratios -> spin eigenvalues of the three-body Gram operator
- **r-coupling** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-coupling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-coupling): Raising equation, orthogonal change of variables and normalized lowering induction -> limiting coupled polynomials
- **r-compression-lemma** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-compression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-compression): Equality of quadratic forms on the range, norm bound of annihilators -> compression equivalence and annihilator bound
- **r-transfer** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-transfer): Diagonal change of variables to a fixed local space and perturbation of finite matrices -> uniform transfer to finite flux
- **r-four-body** [claim](#stmt-rb-prop-a4) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-prop-a4): Haar averaging of matrix units and Bessel inequality on orthonormal highest vectors -> four-body estimate with explicit allowance
- **r-three-body-tail** [claim](#stmt-rb-lem-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-tail): Highest-vector orbit resolution and dominated convergence with geometric series -> three-body tail bound and its limit
- **r-retained** [claim](#stmt-prop-retained-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-rb-lem-tail-2): Combine normal-ordered square, three-body and four-body comparisons with the positive comparison operator -> retained-block inequality
- **r-descent** [claim](#stmt-thm-local-energy-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail-2): Jump operators from pair removal followed by the emptying channel, averaged over rotations -> local energy descent
- **r-number-control** [claim](#stmt-prop-grand-canonical-coercivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-grand-canonical-coercivity): Lindblad evolution, number identity and limit points supported on the kernel -> particle number control and coercivity
- **r-relative-bound** [claim](#stmt-prop-relative-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-relative-bound): Closed quadratic inequality for the best constant using the loss evolution and the zero-mode bound -> relative form bound
- **r-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-40): Spectral transport of the ground line, termwise centering, relative bound and bootstrap over the disorder interval -> uniform gap and uniqueness under weak scalar disorder

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-final-margin**: The exact rational coefficient of H left after subtracting the two-body coefficient, the three-body tail limit and the four-body allowance equals 4616733319001/10^14, which exceeds 1/25.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-margin-as-finite-q**: Read the checked rational margin as a gap bound valid at every finite flux. -> The margin is a limiting coefficient; the proposition needs q large enough for the coefficient to exceed the chosen constant.
- **anti-arithmetic-not-proof**: Treat agreement of the final subtraction as verification of the whole retained-block proof. -> The subtraction takes the row data, three-body traces and four-body positivity checks as given and does not re-derive them.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The final margin in the retained-block proof is a finite exact rational computation, so it can be recomputed and compared with the stated value and with 1/25. The analytic results of the paper are not finite and are not covered.

```python
# INPUTS: one line of five integers a n d k e (numerator of the two-body coefficient over 10^14, numerator and denominator of the tail limit, multiplier and numerator over 10^6)
# PRECONDITIONS: d is a positive integer
# OUTPUTS: one line: the reduced fraction of the margin, then YES if it exceeds 1/25 else NO
# TIME: constant number of big-integer operations
# SPACE: constant
# ARITHMETIC: exact rational arithmetic, no floating point
# FAILURE: d equal to zero raises ZeroDivisionError and the run exits nonzero
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
a, n, d, k, e = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
m = 1 - Fraction(a, 10**14) - Fraction(n, d) - Fraction(k*e, 10**6)
print(str(m.numerator) + '/' + str(m.denominator) + ' ' + ('YES' if m > Fraction(1,25) else 'NO'))
```

```json
        "input": "0 0 1 0 0"
      },
      {

        "input": "200000000000000 0 1 0 0"
      },
      {
        "expected": "0/1 NO",
        "input": "0 0 1 1000 1000"
      }
    ]
  },

}
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves existential statements about a quantum Hamiltonian on a sphere. Its only standing convention for constants is that they do not depend on flux or particle number, which gives no program interface to implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The loss evolution is a finite-dimensional Lindblad generator used as a proof device. It is not a simulation scheme and no step size or integrator is given. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The unperturbed gap theorem is cited from a companion manuscript and comes with an explicit rational constant. The paper itself provides no checker or tool for it, so there is nothing to turn into a tool. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Only the appendix certificate is a finite procedure. It forms exact rational matrices and checks positivity through a trace recurrence for the characteristic polynomial. The main stability theorem itself has no algorithm. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The localized frame has a bounded-overlap property, but it is an analytic device for proving locality estimates. The paper does not describe it as an indexing or storage structure. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The Fock-space lift is an algebraic map that preserves positivity and rotations. No arithmetic precision, memory layout or parallel scheme is specified for hardware. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The sphere carries a fixed number of flux quanta and the result is about a spectral gap of a physical model. There is no hardness assumption, adversary or protocol. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The potential is a fixed bounded real function, not learned data. The amplitude-only formulation concerns Toeplitz multiplication and gives no statistical or training guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The quadratic inequality for the best relative-bound constant is a step in a proof that yields an existential bound. It is not an optimisation problem with a solver or objective. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The commutation of even operators on disjoint sets is a fact about fermionic algebras. It does not model message passing, consensus or fault tolerance. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is mathematical physics: certifying robustness of the Laughlin gap under weak one-body disorder. This is a theory result, with no engineering construction beyond the finite certificate. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-existential-constants** (nonconstructive): The disorder threshold and the gap are existential constants; the paper does not give them explicitly or optimize them. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem to certify a numerical disorder strength at which the gap is still open for a concrete system', 'Quoting a specific value of the threshold or of the gap for the perturbed model']
- **lim-laughlin-flux-setting** (hypothesis): The model is fixed to the Laughlin flux relation on a round sphere, with the particle number at least two. Blocked use: ['Applying the stability statement to a flux that is not three times one less than the particle number', 'Transferring the result to a different filling fraction or a non-spherical geometry']
- **lim-imported-unperturbed-gap** (formal_scope): The unperturbed Fock-space gap is taken from a companion manuscript and is only recalled here, so everything downstream rests on that import. Blocked use: ['Treating the stability theorem as self-contained without the companion gap result', 'Claiming the unperturbed gap is re-proved in this paper']
- **lim-large-flux-threshold** (hypothesis): The unperturbed coercivity holds only above a flux threshold that depends on the chosen constant below the limiting value. Blocked use: ['Applying the coercivity inequality at small or moderate flux', 'Assuming one threshold serves every admissible constant']
- **lim-zero-sector-range** (edge_case): Zero modes exist only in sectors whose particle number does not exceed the filled value, and the filled sector is the single Laughlin line. Blocked use: ['Expecting zero-energy states with excess particles above Laughlin filling', 'Using the zero-mode bound in an empty sector as if it carried content']
- **lim-local-linear-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The linear dependence of local expectations on the electron deficit is proved only for neutral Hermitian interactions with uniform center density and uniform rapid locality bounds at Laughlin flux. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to non-neutral or non-local observables', 'Applying it to interactions whose locality or density constants grow with flux']
- **lim-loss-evolution-stage** (formal_scope): The particle-loss jumps remove a bounded number of particles each; the construction is a proof device and the loss is bounded by a fixed cutoff. Blocked use: ['Reading the loss generator as a validated simulation scheme', 'Assuming jumps that remove an arbitrary number of particles']
- **lim-relative-bound-centering** (hypothesis): The relative form bound requires each local term to be physical, neutral, Hermitian, to annihilate the ground vector, and to have bounded local norm at a fixed order. Blocked use: ['Bounding uncentered local terms by the coercive operator', 'Applying the bound to terms with unbounded local norms']
- **lim-transport-bootstrap** (hypothesis): Spectral transport is run on a connected parameter interval about zero where the ground state in the filled sector is simple and the gap stays above a fixed fraction of the reference gap. Blocked use: ['Following the ground line across a point where the gap closes', 'Using the transport outside the bootstrap interval']
- **lim-retained-block-threshold** (approximation): The retained-block inequality holds above a flux threshold for each fixed constant below the limit; the checked rational margin is a limiting coefficient, not a finite-flux bound. Blocked use: ['Reading the rational margin as a gap valid at every finite flux', 'Using a constant at or above the limit']
- **lim-exact-arithmetic-certificate** (formal_scope): The finite certificate is valid only when the matrices are real symmetric and computed with exact fractions throughout. Blocked use: ['Running the positivity recurrence in floating point', 'Applying the coefficient-sign test to non-symmetric matrices']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 269 (`lean/docs/269.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Uniform Stability of the Spherical Laughlin Gap](../../preprints/Uniform-Stability-of-the-Spherical-Laughlin-Gap-October-5-2026/uniform-stability-spherical-laughlin-gap.pdf)
> - [A Fock-space inequality and the Laughlin spectral gap](../../preprints/A-Fock-space-inequality-and-the-Laughlin-spectral-gap-September-24-2026/A-Fock-space-inequality-and-the-Laughlin-spectral-gap-September-24-2026.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The linked formalization proves the uniform unperturbed gap estimate used in the paper's stability argument for the fermionic Laughlin state at filling $1/3$ on the sphere. At flux $q=3(N-1)$ and all sufficiently large particle numbers $N$, every antisymmetric state has $V_1$ energy at least $1/25$ times its squared distance from the Laughlin ground-state line.
>
> This selected statement is the unperturbed Fock-space inequality. Stability under projected one-body potentials and uniqueness of the perturbed ground state are outside it.
>
> The Laughlin spectral-gap problem asks for a positive gap that remains uniform as the system grows. The formalization includes the finite spherical $V_1$ bound of $1/100$ above the Laughlin state at flux $3(N-1)$ for all sufficiently large particle numbers $N$. It also proves the stronger Fock-space inequality $H_Q^2\ge\gamma H_Q$ for every fixed $0<\gamma<\gamma_*$ and all sufficiently large fluxes $Q$, independently of particle number, where $\gamma_*=4616733319001/10^{14}>1/25$.
>
> For the untruncated planar model, the formalization proves the corresponding inequality at the endpoint $\gamma_*$ on every homogeneous particle sector. These statements use coefficient one for each pair projector.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace Laughlin
open scoped BigOperators

abbrev Configuration (N Q : ℕ) := Fin N → Fin (Q+1)
abbrev State (N Q : ℕ) := Configuration N Q → ℂ

 
def Antisymmetric {N Q : ℕ} (ψ : State N Q) : Prop :=
  ∀ (i j : Fin N), i ≠ j → ∀ a, ψ (a ∘ Equiv.swap i j) = -ψ a

 
noncomputable def pairCoefficient (Q p : ℕ) (x y : Fin (Q+1)) : ℝ :=
  if x.val + y.val = p+1 then
    ((x.val : ℝ) - (y.val : ℝ)) *
      Real.sqrt (((Q.descFactorial x.val : ℝ) * (Q.descFactorial y.val : ℝ) *
        (p.factorial : ℝ)) /
        ((Q : ℝ) * ((2*Q-2).descFactorial p : ℝ) *
          (x.val.factorial : ℝ) * (y.val.factorial : ℝ))) / Real.sqrt 2
  else 0

 

noncomputable def pairAmplitude {N Q : ℕ} (ψ : State N Q)
    (i j : Fin N) (p : ℕ) (a : Configuration N Q) : ℂ :=
  ∑ x : Fin (Q+1), ∑ y : Fin (Q+1),
    (pairCoefficient Q p x y : ℂ) * ψ (Function.update (Function.update a i x) j y)

 

noncomputable def energy {N Q : ℕ} (ψ : State N Q) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i : Fin N, ∑ j : Fin N, if i < j then
    ∑ p ∈ Finset.range (2*Q-1), ∑ a : Configuration N Q,
      if a i = 0 ∧ a j = 0 then ‖pairAmplitude ψ i j p a‖ ^ 2 else 0
  else 0

abbrev SpinorVariables (N : ℕ) := Fin N × Bool

 
noncomputable def bracket {N : ℕ} (i j : Fin N) : MvPolynomial (SpinorVariables N) ℂ :=
  MvPolynomial.X (i,false) * MvPolynomial.X (j,true) -
    MvPolynomial.X (j,false) * MvPolynomial.X (i,true)

 
noncomputable def laughlinPolynomial (N : ℕ) : MvPolynomial (SpinorVariables N) ℂ :=
  ∏ i : Fin N, ∏ j : Fin N, if i < j then bracket i j ^ 3 else 1

 
noncomputable def monomialExponent {N Q : ℕ} (a : Configuration N Q) : SpinorVariables N →₀ ℕ :=
  Finsupp.onFinset Finset.univ
    (fun ib => if ib.2 then (a ib.1).val else Q - (a ib.1).val)
    (by intro; simp)

 

noncomputable def laughlinVector (N Q : ℕ) : State N Q := fun a =>
  (laughlinPolynomial N).coeff (monomialExponent a) /
    ((∏ i : Fin N, Real.sqrt (Nat.choose Q (a i).val : ℝ) : ℝ) : ℂ)

 

noncomputable def distanceToLaughlinSq {N Q : ℕ} (ψ : State N Q) : ℝ :=
  sInf (Set.range (fun c : ℂ => ∑ a : Configuration N Q,
    ‖ψ a - c * laughlinVector N Q a‖ ^ 2))
end Laughlin

namespace LaughlinGap
export _root_.OAI.Laughlin (Configuration State Antisymmetric pairCoefficient pairAmplitude energy SpinorVariables bracket laughlinPolynomial monomialExponent laughlinVector distanceToLaughlinSq)

def MainTarget : Prop :=
  ∃ N₀ : ℕ, 2 ≤ N₀ ∧ ∀ N : ℕ, N₀ ≤ N →
    ∀ ψ : State N (3*(N-1)), Antisymmetric ψ →
      (1/25 : ℝ) * distanceToLaughlinSq ψ ≤ energy ψ

theorem thm_main : MainTarget := by
  sorry

end LaughlinGap

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the fermionic Laughlin <i>V</i><sub>1</sub> Hamiltonian at filling 1/3 on expanding round spheres retains a unique ground state and a uniform spectral gap under sufficiently weak bounded real scalar one-body potentials projected to the lowest Landau level. With coefficient one for each pair projector and Laughlin flux $`q=3(N-1)`$, the gap and perturbation threshold are uniform for all sufficiently large particle numbers and all potential profiles of supremum norm at most one. The result uses the uniform unperturbed Fock-space gap and gives existential constants.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Laughlin’s wave function describes a strongly correlated state at fractional filling of the lowest Landau level <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Laughlin](#ref-Laughlin)]. Haldane’s spherical geometry and pseudopotentials give a particularly simple parent Hamiltonian: each electron pair is penalized by the orthogonal projection onto relative angular momentum one <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Haldane](#ref-Haldane)]. The cubic Laughlin polynomial is its unique zero state at filling $1/3$ with the spherical shift. A spectral gap separates this state from excitations. Stability asks whether that separation survives a weak external potential whose total many-particle norm grows with the area of the system.

We prove uniform stability for the full spherical interaction under bounded scalar potentials projected to the lowest Landau level. The potential may vary with system size and need not have any symmetry. The proof uses the uniform unperturbed Fock-space gap of <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Gap](#ref-Gap)], and derives the additional local estimates needed to pass from a gap at zero disorder to a gap on a fixed disorder interval.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Model and main result

For $N\ge2$, set $q=3(N-1)$ and let $S_q$ be the round sphere of radius $\sqrt{q/2}$. Magnetic length is one, so the sphere carries $q$ flux quanta and has area $2\pi q$. Let $U_q$ be its one-particle lowest Landau level, equivalently the spin-$q/2$ representation of $SU(2)$. On the entire antisymmetric space $\bigwedge^N U_q$, put <a id="eq:intro-H"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-H}
 H_{N,q}=\sum_{1\le i<j\le N}P_{ij}^{(1)},$$ where $P_{ij}^{(1)}$ projects the indicated pair onto total spin $q-1$. Each unordered pair has coefficient one. The normalized Laughlin vector spans the line generated by <a id="eq:intro-laughlin"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-laughlin}
 \Psi_{\mathrm L,N}=\prod_{i<j}
       (z_{i0}z_{j1}-z_{i1}z_{j0})^3.$$

If $\Pi_q$ denotes the lowest-level projection and $\varphi$ is a bounded real scalar function on $S_q$, its Toeplitz operator and many-particle perturbation are $$T_q(\varphi)=(\Pi_qM_\varphi\Pi_q)|_{U_q},\qquad
 V_{N,q}(\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^N T_q(\varphi)^{(i)}.$$ Let $E_0(N,\lambda,\varphi)\le E_1(N,\lambda,\varphi)$ be the two lowest eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, of $$H_{N,q}(\lambda,\varphi)=H_{N,q}+\lambda V_{N,q}(\varphi).$$

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Uniform scalar-disorder stability). *There are constants $\lambda_*>0$, $\Delta_*>0$, and $N_*<\infty$ such that, for every $N\ge N_*$, every real bounded measurable $\varphi$ on $S_{3(N-1)}$ with $\|\varphi\|_\infty\le1$, and every real $|\lambda|\le\lambda_*$, <a id="eq:intro-stability"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-stability}
 E_1(N,\lambda,\varphi)-E_0(N,\lambda,\varphi)\ge\Delta_*.$$ In particular the perturbed ground state is unique.*

</div>

The constants are uniform over the potential as well as particle number. Thus the theorem applies, in particular, to smooth potentials satisfying $\max_{0\le k\le2}\|\nabla^k\varphi\|_\infty\le1$ in the physical round metric. Its stronger amplitude-only formulation comes from the coherent-orbital representation of Toeplitz multiplication. The ground vector is allowed to move, and its energy is subtracted in the stability argument. Adding a constant to the potential changes only that energy. We give existential constants; the proof does not optimize the disorder threshold.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Historical context and the additional difficulty

Exact parent Hamiltonians have been central to the mathematical study of fractional quantum Hall states since the work of Trugman and Kivelson <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[TK](#ref-TK)]. Haldane pseudopotentials also arise from suitable short-range limits of repulsive interactions, as proved by Seiringer and Yngvason <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[SY](#ref-SY)]. The spectral gap is a separate quantitative question: identifying a zero polynomial does not control the energy of vectors perpendicular to it. Rougerie’s survey <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Rougerie](#ref-Rougerie)] discusses the gap problem and its role in the response of the Laughlin phase to perturbations.

Rigorous uniform gaps were established for truncated pseudopotentials in thin-cylinder and thin-torus geometries by Nachtergaele, Warzel, and Young <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[NWY](#ref-NWY)] and Warzel and Young . The unperturbed input used here concerns the full interaction on expanding round spheres <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Gap](#ref-Gap)]. We state its exact normalization in Theorem <a href="#thm:unperturbed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:unperturbed">3</a>. In addition, we use and reproduce a strengthening of its four-body comparison: certain positive blocks that were discarded in obtaining the gap can be retained. This stronger estimate supplies the energy-loss construction below.

Another line of work controls density within the Laughlin-correlated class. Rougerie and Yngvason <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[RY](#ref-RY)], followed by Lieb, Rougerie, and Yngvason <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[LRY](#ref-LRY)], established incompressibility estimates for Laughlin functions multiplied by analytic symmetric factors. The sharp local density bound in <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LRY](#ref-LRY)] is averaged over mesoscopic disks. Our zero-mode estimate controls general local observables, with total cost proportional to the number of missing electrons; it is then combined with an estimate for states outside the zero space. Algebraic descriptions and recursions for pseudopotential zero modes were developed in <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS), [MONS](#ref-MONS)]. We use the symmetric-polynomial description and add quantitative bounds for its orthogonal branching.

General gap-stability theorems explain the role of local information. Bravyi, Hastings, and Michalakis <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BHM](#ref-BHM)] treated stability under sufficiently weak, suitably decaying local perturbations of commuting-projector models with topological-order conditions. Michalakis and Zwolak <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MZ](#ref-MZ)] proved stability for frustration-free lattice Hamiltonians under local topological-order and local-gap hypotheses. Subsequent infinite-volume formulations also retain local conditions in addition to a bulk gap <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[NSYBulk](#ref-NSYBulk)]. Those hypotheses contain more information than a uniform global gap. For the spherical lowest Landau level, the local control needed here is established through flux pinning and a particle-loss evolution. The resulting relative-bound argument has the same purpose as local block diagonalization in the lattice theory, with a different source of control for the diagonal terms.

Our final continuation of the ground line uses the quasiadiabatic construction of Hastings and Wen <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HW](#ref-HW)] and the exact spectral-flow framework of Bachmann, Michalakis, Nachtergaele, and Sims <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BMNS](#ref-BMNS)]. It is applied inside a gap bootstrap, where its gap hypothesis is available. Locality is obtained using redundant localized frames. This connects to the continuum fermionic frame method of Bachmann and De Nittis <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[BDN](#ref-BDN)]; we prove the finite-sphere estimates, including comparisons at neighboring fluxes, that the present argument requires. The propagation estimates follow the Lieb–Robinson method <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[LR](#ref-LR)] and its fermionic form <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[NSY](#ref-NSY)].

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The proof and its reusable estimates

The main difficulty is extensive perturbation size: $\|V_{N,q}(\varphi)\|$ can be of order $N$. A norm bound on the whole perturbation therefore gives a disorder interval that shrinks with particle number. We instead compare suitably centered local terms with the interaction energy. Two estimates make that comparison possible.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Local observables in zero spaces.

<a id="eq:intro-zero-bound"></a>
At fixed flux $q=3(N-1)$, write $Z_{n,q}=\ker H_{n,q}$. For an interaction $W$ that is a bounded-density sum of uniformly localized neutral terms, we prove $$\label{eq:intro-zero-bound}
 \left|\langle\psi,W\psi\rangle
       -\langle\Omega,W\Omega\rangle\right|
 \le C_W(N-n),\qquad
 \psi\in Z_{n,q},\quad\|\psi\|=1,$$ where $\Omega$ is the normalized filled Laughlin vector. This is Theorem <a href="#thm:charge-local-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:charge-local-linear">33</a>; the constant is uniform in flux. Neutral means that a term preserves particle number; localization is defined precisely in Section <a href="#sec:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:locality">3</a>. The linear dependence on $N-n$ is essential.

To prove <a href="#eq:intro-zero-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-zero-bound">[eq:intro-zero-bound]</a>, we read a zero mode from one pole. Either the last orbital is empty, which removes one unit of flux and records a flag, or contraction in that orbital removes an electron and three units of flux. The branches are orthogonal and give coordinates for the entire zero space. The number of flags is the *quasihole degree* $h=q+3-3n$. At the filled flux it equals $3(N-n)$, three times the electron deficit; a one-hole sector below means $h=1$, not one missing electron. Pinning zeros at the pole realizes the required change of flux. The uniform Fock gap persists along that pinning deformation and gives local unitary transport. A history of branch choices then bounds a local observable by weighted flag values along the history. A first, slowly decaying weight supplies enough information to close a second estimate with an integrable weight. Rotation averaging yields <a href="#eq:intro-zero-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-zero-bound">[eq:intro-zero-bound]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Local descent of interaction energy.

Extend the pair Hamiltonian to all particle sectors by $H_q=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{q+1}H_{n,q}$, with $H_{0,q}=H_{1,q}=0$, and let $\mathcal N$ act as multiplication by $n$ on the $n$-particle sector. On this Fock space we construct local particle-loss operators $J_y$ such that (Theorem <a href="#thm:local-energy-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:local-energy-descent">34</a>) <a id="eq:intro-descent"></a>$$\label{eq:intro-descent}
 \int J_y^*J_y\,dy=H_q,\qquad
 \int J_y^*H_qJ_y\,dy\le H_q^2-cH_q.$$ Each jump removes between two and a fixed number of particles. The associated completely positive evolution decreases interaction energy exponentially, and its expected particle loss is bounded by the initial energy. The construction first removes a pair and then partially empties a fixed neighborhood. A rotation average shows that the remaining pair correlations are paid for by the retained four-body blocks. This also proves a uniform energy cost for particle excess above Laughlin filling.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### From descent to perturbation theory.

For a small fixed $\mu>0$, the operator $G=H_q+\mu(N-\mathcal N)$ controls both interaction energy and particle-number deficit or excess. If each neutral local term $A_x$ annihilates $\Omega$, following its expectation through the loss evolution gives $$\pm\sum_xA_x\le CG.$$ The proof closes a quadratic inequality for the best relative-bound constant, using <a href="#eq:intro-zero-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-zero-bound">[eq:intro-zero-bound]</a> at the limiting zero state (Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-bound">38</a>). This implication from local energy descent and linear electron-deficit control is useful independently of the particular flux construction. Finally spectral transport makes the derivative of the pulled-back perturbed Hamiltonian termwise centered in this sense. On the $N$-particle sector $G=H_q$, so integration gives a uniform lower bound above the transported ground state.

Section <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a> fixes the algebra and zero-mode conventions. Sections <a href="#sec:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:locality">3</a>–<a href="#sec:charge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:charge">6</a> establish the local zero-mode estimate. Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">7</a> constructs the loss evolution, and Section <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">8</a> proves the relative bound and completes Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Appendix <a href="#app:retained-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:retained-blocks">9</a> contains the retained-block comparison and its finite arithmetic data.

<a id="sec:prelim"></a>
### Lowest Landau level and zero modes

We fix the normalization of the interaction before discussing locality. The exterior-algebra formulation is useful because both the pinned Hamiltonians and the particle-loss evolution change particle number. Throughout the paper, constants are independent of flux and particle number unless an explicit dependence is indicated. A statement for large flux means that its threshold is independent of the particle sector.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Coherent orbitals and pair annihilators

Let $q\ge1$ be an integer. The sphere $S_q$ has radius $\sqrt{q/2}$, area $2\pi q$, and magnetic field one. The lowest Landau level $U_q$ is the spin-$q/2$ representation of $SU(2)$. We use homogeneous polynomials of degree $q$ in spinor coordinates $(z_0,z_1)$, with invariant inner product normalized so that $$e_j=\binom qj^{1/2}z_0^{q-j}z_1^j,
 \qquad 0\le j\le q,$$ is an orthonormal basis. This normalization identifies $U_q$ unitarily with the physical lowest Landau level; it does not rescale any operator. Relative to a chosen north pole, $j=0$ is the north orbital and $j=q$ the south orbital.

<a id="eq:toeplitz-resolution"></a>
Write $k_x$ for the unit coherent vector at $x\in S_q$, defined up to a phase by the Riesz vector for evaluation at $x$. Its components in the axis basis have squared moduli <a id="eq:binomial-coherent"></a>$$\label{eq:binomial-coherent}
 |\langle e_j,k_x\rangle|^2
 =\binom qj (1-m/q)^{q-j}(m/q)^j,
 \qquad m=q\sin^2(\theta/2).$$ Schur’s Lemma and the trace give the resolution <a id="eq:coherent-resolution"></a>$$\label{eq:coherent-resolution}
 \kappa_q\int_{S_q}|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|\,dA(x)=I_{U_q},
 \qquad \kappa_q=\frac{q+1}{2\pi q}.$$ In particular the density $\kappa_q$ is uniformly bounded. For a bounded measurable real function $\varphi$, the Toeplitz operator is $$\label{eq:toeplitz-resolution}
 T_q(\varphi)=\kappa_q\int_{S_q}
       \varphi(x)|k_x\rangle\langle k_x|\,dA(x).$$ Indeed the quadratic form on the right is the integral of $\varphi$ times the product of the two evaluated lowest-level sections, which is the quadratic form of compression of multiplication by $\varphi$.

<a id="eq:lift"></a>
The fermionic Fock space and number operator are $$\mathcal F_q=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{q+1}\bigwedge^n U_q,
 \qquad \mathcal N\big|_{\wedge^n U_q}=nI.$$ Increasing wedges of orthonormal vectors have norm one. For $a\in\bigwedge^k U_q$, let $B(a)$ be the adjoint of left wedge multiplication by $a$. Thus $B$ is conjugate-linear, $B(a\wedge b)=B(b)B(a)$, and $c_j=B(e_j)$ satisfy the canonical anticommutation relations. For a one-particle vector $f$ we also write $c(f)=B(f)$. For an operator on $\bigwedge^k U_q$, its lift to Fock space is the linear extension of $$\label{eq:lift}
 \mathcal L_k(|a\rangle\langle b|)=B(a)^*B(b).$$ The lift preserves positivity and rotations. It vanishes on sectors with fewer than $k$ particles and equals its argument on the $k$-particle sector.

<a id="eq:pair-rows"></a>
<a id="eq:fock-H"></a>
Let $V_q\subset\bigwedge^2 U_q$ be the spin-$(q-1)$ summand. Its orthonormal axis basis $v_p$, $0\le p\le2q-2$, can be written as $$\label{eq:pair-rows}
 v_{b-1}=Z_{q,b}^{-1/2}
 \sum_{\substack{0\le i<j\le q\\i+j=b}}
 (j-i)\binom qi^{1/2}\binom qj^{1/2}e_i\wedge e_j,
 \quad
 Z_{q,b}=\frac{b(2q-b)}{2(2q-1)}\binom{2q}{b},$$ where $1\le b\le2q-1$. The formula follows by lowering the highest vector $e_0\wedge e_1$. For its normalization, the sum over ordered $i,j$ with $i+j=b$ is a hypergeometric second moment: its total mass is $\binom{2q}{b}$ and the mean of $(j-i)^2$ is $b(2q-b)/(2q-1)$. Dividing by two gives $Z_{q,b}$. We set $B_p=B(v_p)$. The interaction is $$\label{eq:fock-H}
 H_q=\mathcal L_2(\Pi_{V_q})=
 \sum_{p=0}^{2q-2}B_p^*B_p,
 \qquad H_{n,q}=H_q\big|_{\wedge^n U_q}.$$ On $n$ particles this is exactly $\sum_{i<j}P_{ij}^{(1)}$, with coefficient one for each unordered pair. One way to check the coefficient is to identify a unit wedge with its normalized antisymmetric tensor: contraction in two prescribed tensor positions is $B(a)/\sqrt{\binom n2}$, and summing the $\binom n2$ pair projections gives <a href="#eq:fock-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fock-H">[eq:fock-H]</a>.

<a id="eq:wedge-maps"></a>
We shall also use the wedge maps $$\label{eq:wedge-maps}
 \begin{aligned}
 W_3 &: V_q\otimes U_q\longrightarrow\bigwedge^3 U_q,
       &W_3(a\otimes u)&=a\wedge u,\\
 W_4 &: V_q\otimes V_q\longrightarrow\bigwedge^4 U_q,
       &W_4(a\otimes b)&=a\wedge b.
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular $V_q\otimes V_q$ uses ordered pairs. This convention fixes the coefficient of the four-body term in the normal-ordered square used in Appendix <a href="#app:retained-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:retained-blocks">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The zero spaces and the unperturbed input

The polynomial and second-quantized descriptions of these kernels are closely related; see <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS), [MONS](#ref-MONS)] for algebraic constructions of pseudopotential zero modes. Put $Z_{n,q}=\ker H_{n,q}$ and let $P_{n,q}$ be its orthogonal projection. When all sectors are being considered, write $Z_q=\ker H_q$ and $P_q$ for the Fock-space zero projection.

<a id="stmt-lem-zero-modes"></a>
<div id="lem:zero-modes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:zero-polynomial"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Polynomial description). *For $n\ge1$, a vector lies in $Z_{n,q}$ if and only if its polynomial has the form $$\label{eq:zero-polynomial}
 \prod_{i<j}(z_{i0}z_{j1}-z_{i1}z_{j0})^3\,S(z_1,\ldots,z_n),$$ where $S$ is symmetric and homogeneous of degree $h=q+3-3n$ in each spinor. Consequently $Z_{n,q}=\{0\}$ when $h<0$, and, when $h\ge0$, $$\dim Z_{n,q}=\binom{n+h}{n}.$$ At $q=3(N-1)$ the space $Z_{N,q}$ is the Laughlin line, and every nonempty zero sector has particle number at most $N$. The vacuum sector $Z_{0,q}$ has dimension one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-zero-modes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-zero-modes</summary>

*Proof.* Positivity of the pair terms says that a zero vector has no pair relative-angular-momentum-one component. An antisymmetric polynomial of the same homogeneous degree in each of two spinors vanishes when the spinors coincide. Separate homogeneity extends that vanishing to proportional spinors, so the irreducible bracket $[z_1,z_2]$ divides it. Dividing by the bracket and then setting $z_1=z_2=z$ gives an equivariant map to the degree-$(2q-2)$ polynomials in $z$, the spin-$(q-1)$ representation. This map is nonzero on $e_0\wedge e_1$ and hence is onto. Its kernel is exactly divisibility by the bracket cubed: the divided polynomial is symmetric and separately homogeneous, and if it vanishes on the diagonal it is divisible by the bracket; the resulting quotient is antisymmetric and therefore divisible by the bracket once more. By the two-spin decomposition, the map detects precisely the spin-$(q-1)$ summand. Thus absence of that component is equivalent to cubic divisibility.

It follows that every pair bracket cubed divides the polynomial. The distinct pair brackets are nonassociate irreducible polynomials; unique factorization makes their product divide it. Dividing by the cubic product changes antisymmetry to symmetry and leaves degree $q-3(n-1)$ in each variable. This proves both directions of <a href="#eq:zero-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-polynomial">[eq:zero-polynomial]</a>. A symmetric polynomial with individual degree $h$ has the symmetrized monomial basis indexed by $0\le j_1\le\cdots\le j_n\le h$, giving the binomial dimension. The remaining claims follow at once, with the vacuum treated separately. ◻

</details>

The quantitative unperturbed result used in this paper is the following Fock-space gap theorem. We recall it with the normalization just fixed; its proof is in the companion manuscript <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Gap](#ref-Gap), Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2].

<a id="stmt-thm-unperturbed"></a>
<div id="thm:unperturbed" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:accepted-fock-gap"></a>
**Theorem 3** (Uniform unperturbed gap). *Let $\gamma_*=4616733319001/10^{14}$. For every $0<\gamma<\gamma_*$ there is $q_\gamma$ such that $$\label{eq:accepted-fock-gap}
 H_q^2\ge\gamma H_q\qquad(q\ge q_\gamma)$$ on all of $\mathcal F_q$. In particular, for all sufficiently large $N$ and $q=3(N-1)$, <a id="eq:accepted-filled-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:accepted-filled-gap}
 H_{N,q}\ge\frac1{25}(I-P_{N,q}).$$*

</div>

The all-sector statement gives separation of the spectrum from zero. We use it for zero-space transport, including particle numbers below Laughlin filling. The extra four-body comparison needed for energy descent is proved separately in Appendix <a href="#app:retained-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:retained-blocks">9</a>; it is stronger than <a href="#eq:accepted-fock-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:accepted-fock-gap">[eq:accepted-fock-gap]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Geometric scales

An axis on $S_q$ gives latitude coordinate $m=q\sin^2(\theta/2)$ and longitude $\phi$. In these coordinates $dA=dm\,d\phi$. We use <a id="eq:geometric-scales"></a>$$\label{eq:geometric-scales}
 u=1+m,\qquad v=1+q-m,\qquad
 w=\sqrt{\frac{uv}{q+1}}.$$ Here $v$ is an end-distance weight; the potential is always denoted by $\varphi$. The width $w$ converts index distance to physical distance, including in the polar caps. Section <a href="#sec:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:locality">3</a> proves the geometric estimates that make these scales useful for local operators.

<a id="sec:locality"></a>
### A local calculus in the lowest Landau level

The orbital basis diagonalizes rotations about an axis, but its middle orbitals extend around an entire latitude. We first replace that basis by a redundant family localized in both latitude and longitude. Its analysis map embeds the physical fermions into a larger collection of fermionic modes with a genuine notion of spatial support. The use of localized frames to obtain continuum fermionic locality is developed in <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BDN](#ref-BDN)]; we give the finite-sphere construction and estimates needed here. We then prove the propagation and functional-calculus estimates needed to manipulate local operators. All constants in this section are independent of the flux and the particle number.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Localized frames and auxiliary fermions

<a id="eq:local-scale-ratios"></a>
Fix an axis and write <a id="eq:local-scales"></a>$$m=q\sin^2(\theta/2),\qquad u=1+m,\qquad v=1+q-m,
 \qquad w_q(m)=\left(\frac{uv}{q+1}\right)^{1/2}.
 \label{eq:local-scales}$$ The area form is $dm\,d\phi$. Away from the two polar balls of fixed radius, changing $m$ by $w_q(m)$ or changing $\phi$ by $1/w_q(m)$ moves a bounded physical distance. The following elementary comparisons will also be used at the poles, with $u,v\geq1$: $$\frac{u(x)}{u(y)}+\frac{v(x)}{v(y)}
 +\frac{w_q(x)}{w_q(y)}
 \leq C(1+d(x,y))^2.
 \label{eq:local-scale-ratios}$$ Indeed $m$ is one half the squared chordal distance from the north pole; the triangle inequality gives the assertion for $u$, and the south pole gives it for $v$. The assertion for $w_q$ follows from their product.

<a id="eq:frame-windows"></a>
Here is an explicit frame construction. Choose a nonnegative smooth function $\eta$, supported in $(-2,2)$ and positive on $[-1,1]$, and a smooth function $0\leq\chi\leq1$ on $[0,1]$ equal to one on $[0,1/2]$ and zero on $[2/3,1]$. For positive integers $k$ use the nonzero functions $$\chi(j/q)\eta(\sqrt{1+j}-k),\qquad
 (1-\chi(j/q))\eta(\sqrt{1+q-j}-k),\qquad 0\leq j\leq q.$$ Divide each by the square root of the sum of their squares. The resulting windows $\xi_a$ satisfy $$\sum_a\xi_a(j)^2=1,\qquad
\operatorname{supp}\xi_a\subset I_a,\qquad
 |I_a|\leq Cw_a,\qquad
 |\Delta^l\xi_a(j)|\leq C_lw_a^{-l},
 \label{eq:frame-windows}$$ where $I_a$ is an interval of integers, $m_a$ is the corresponding window center, and $w_a=w_q(m_a)$. At the first few windows the constants absorb the bounded widths. Every index belongs to a bounded number of intervals. To check the difference bound, extend each expression smoothly in $j$. On a northern window $k\asymp\sqrt{1+j}\asymp w_q(j)$; each derivative of $\sqrt{1+j}$ costs at least $w_q(j)^{-1}$, and derivatives of $\chi(j/q)$ cost $q^{-1}\leq Cw_q(j)^{-1}$. The denominator is bounded above and below, and obeys the same estimates. The southern windows have the identical verification.

<a id="eq:frame-atoms"></a>
<a id="eq:tight-frame"></a>
<a id="eq:slot-volume"></a>
For a window bearing the index $k$, choose the fixed integer $L_a=16(k+1)$, which is larger than the diameter of the full bump support before restriction to $[0,q]$. Thus $L_a\asymp w_a$ and, for a northern window, its Fourier length is independent of $q$. For $0\leq\ell<L_a$ set $$z_{a\ell}=L_a^{-1/2}\sum_{j=0}^q
       \xi_a(j)e^{2\pi i j\ell/L_a}e_j,
 \qquad x_{a\ell}=(m_a,2\pi\ell/L_a),
 \label{eq:frame-atoms}$$ where $e_j$ is the normalized orbital of Section <a href="#sec:prelim" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prelim">2</a>. Repeated points near the poles are retained as distinct slots. Their multiplicities are bounded. Write $\Lambda_q$ for this set of slots. Fourier orthogonality and <a href="#eq:frame-windows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-windows">[eq:frame-windows]</a> give the exact identity $$\sum_{a,\ell}|z_{a\ell}\rangle\langle z_{a\ell}|=I_{U_q}.
 \label{eq:tight-frame}$$ Thus $\mathsf S_q\psi=(\langle z_x,\psi\rangle)_{x\in\Lambda_q}$ is an isometry from $U_q$ to $\ell^2(\Lambda_q)$. The slots have bounded density: $$\#\{x\in\Lambda_q:d(x,y)\leq r\}\leq C(1+r)^2.
 \label{eq:slot-volume}$$ The window centers have bounded density in radial physical distance, and their circles carry $O(w_a)$ slots with spacing bounded below except inside the bounded polar balls. This proves <a href="#eq:slot-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slot-volume">[eq:slot-volume]</a>. Figure <a href="#fig:local-slots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:local-slots">1</a> shows the two scales in this construction.

<figure id="fig:local-slots">

<figcaption>The index window has width comparable to $w_{a}$; its Fourier phases place a comparable number of slots around the latitude. Together with unit-order radial spacing, this gives bounded slot density in physical distance. The drawing is schematic: dashed circles indicate rapidly decaying tails, not compact spatial support.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-local-slots"></a>

```latex
     (.3,.35)..controls (1,.35) and (1.05,1.65)..(2.15,1.65)
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
- Panel (a) labeled "(a) A window in orbital index": A horizontal axis with an arrow pointing right labeled $j$. A bell-shaped curve representing a window is plotted above the axis, with peak labeled $\xi_a$ centered at position $m_a$. Labels below indicate "index width $O(w_a)$" and "$L_a \asymp w_a$ Fourier phases". An arrow points from panel (a) to panel (b).
- Panel (b) labeled "(b) Slots on a physical latitude": An ellipse depicting a latitude circle with several dashed circles distributed around it representing slots ("rapidly localized physical atom" indicated by an arrow pointing to one circle). Labels below state "angular spacing $O(w_a^{-1})$" and "physical spacing $O(1)$".
```


We need two quantitative consequences of this construction. Let $k_y$ be a normalized coherent orbital centered at $y$.

<a id="stmt-locality-frame"></a>
<div id="locality:frame" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:frame-coherent-decay"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Uniform frame localization). *For every $D$ there is $C_D$ such that $$|\langle z_x,k_y\rangle|\leq C_D(1+d(x,y))^{-D}.
 \label{eq:frame-coherent-decay}$$ For two frames constructed as above, possibly with different axes, their atoms satisfy the same bound with $k_y$ replaced by an atom centered at $y$. For each fixed integer $J$, <a href="#eq:frame-coherent-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-coherent-decay">[eq:frame-coherent-decay]</a> also holds, with constants depending on $J$, for every rotated polar orbital $e_j(y)$ with $0\leq j\leq J$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-frame"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-frame</summary>

<a id="eq:binomial-symbol"></a>
<a id="eq:binomial-global-tail"></a>
*Proof.* Choose the axial gauge and azimuth convention in which the coefficient of $e_j$ in $k_y$ is $e^{ij\phi_y}p_{q,m}(j)^{1/2}$. Its modulus is $$p_{q,m}(j)^{1/2},\qquad
 p_{q,m}(j)=\binom qj(m/q)^j(1-m/q)^{q-j}.$$ They obey, after extension by zero outside $[0,q]$, $$|\Delta^l p_{q,m}(j)^{1/2}|
 \leq C_{D,l}w_q(m)^{-1/2-l}
       \left(1+\frac{|j-m|}{w_q(m)}\right)^{-D}.
 \label{eq:binomial-symbol}$$ Here and below finite differences may shift the argument by a fixed number of indices, which only changes the constant. We include a verification to specify uniformity near the ends. First put $p=m/q$ and $\sigma^2=qp(1-p)$, so $w_q(m)^2=1+q\sigma^2/(q+1)\asymp1+\sigma^2$. Fourier inversion of a binomial law of variance $\sigma^2$ gives $$\sup_j p_{q,m}(j)\leq\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
       \bigl(1-4p(1-p)\sin^2(t/2)\bigr)^{q/2}\,dt
       \leq \frac C{1+\sigma}.$$ For $0<p<1$, tilt the law by $e^{\lambda j}$. Its new parameter is $p_\lambda=pe^\lambda/(1-p+pe^\lambda)$, and its variance differs from $\sigma^2$ by a factor between $e^{-|\lambda|}$ and $e^{|\lambda|}$. Therefore the centered log moment generating function satisfies $\log\mathbb E e^{\lambda(X-m)}\leq\sigma^2\lambda^2$ for $|\lambda|\leq1/2$. At $z=j-m$, choose $\lambda=z/(2(\sigma^2+|z|))$. The exact tilting identity and the preceding point-mass bound give $$p_{q,m}(j)\leq\frac C{w_q(m)}
       \exp\left(-\frac{c|j-m|^2}{w_q(m)^2+|j-m|}\right).
 \label{eq:binomial-global-tail}$$ The cases $p=0,1$ are direct. This proves every polynomial tail bound with the required height, uniformly also when $w_q(m)$ is bounded.

For the derivative gain first assume $w_q(m)^2\geq16(l+1)$, and let $h=\min(m,q-m)\asymp w_q(m)^2$. Interpolate the square root of the binomial probability by gamma functions, writing the resulting positive function as $F(x)$. Put $\psi=(\log\Gamma)'$ and $\psi_1=(\log\Gamma)''$. On $|x-m|\leq h/2$, the explicit formulas $$\begin{aligned}
 (\log F)'(x)=\tfrac12\left(\log\frac p{1-p}
          -\psi(x+1)+\psi(q-x+1)\right),\\
 (\log F)''(x)=-\tfrac12\bigl(\psi_1(x+1)+\psi_1(q-x+1)\bigr)
 \end{aligned}$$ and the positive-real polygamma series give $|(\log F)'(m)|\leq Cw^{-2}$, $(\log F)''(x)\leq-cw^{-2}$, and $|(\log F)^{(k)}(x)|\leq C_kw^{-k}$ for $k\geq2$. Stirling’s inequalities give $F(m)\leq Cw^{-1/2}$. Differentiating $F=e^{\log F}$ consequently proves $$|F^{(l)}(x)|\leq C_{D,l}w^{-1/2-l}
                    (1+|x-m|/w)^{-D},\qquad |x-m|\leq h/2.$$ If $|j-m|\leq h/4$, integrate this derivative over the unit cube that represents $\Delta^lF(j)$; the entire stencil stays in the displayed interval. Outside that region, each nonzero stencil entry has $|j+a-m|\geq cw^2$, $0\leq a\leq l$. Equation <a href="#eq:binomial-global-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-global-tail">[eq:binomial-global-tail]</a> then supplies an exponential in that distance, which pays both $w^{-l}$ and every polynomial tail. This also handles stencils crossing an endpoint. Finally, if $w^2<16(l+1)$, bound the finite difference by its $l+1$ values and use <a href="#eq:binomial-global-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-global-tail">[eq:binomial-global-tail]</a>; the factor $w^{-l}$ is now a constant depending only on $l$. This completes the proof of <a href="#eq:binomial-symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-symbol">[eq:binomial-symbol]</a>, including the bounded-width regime.

Insert <a href="#eq:binomial-symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-symbol">[eq:binomial-symbol]</a> in <a href="#eq:frame-atoms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-atoms">[eq:frame-atoms]</a>. For windows whose radial centers are a bounded distance apart, their widths are comparable; summation by parts $l$ times gives the factor $(1+w_a|\phi_x-\phi_y|)^{-l}$, with angular difference modulo $2\pi$. The absolute sum before summation by parts is bounded. For separated radial windows, the tail in <a href="#eq:binomial-symbol" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-symbol">[eq:binomial-symbol]</a> first supplies arbitrary powers of radial distance. The ratios of the widths cost only polynomial powers by <a href="#eq:local-scale-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-scale-ratios">[eq:local-scale-ratios]</a>. Spending additional tail orders therefore gives the same angular estimate. Radial distance plus the shorter latitude arc bounds the spherical distance from above, proving <a href="#eq:frame-coherent-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frame-coherent-decay">[eq:frame-coherent-decay]</a>.

The coherent resolution of the identity has density $(q+1)/(2\pi q)$ with respect to area. Inserting it between two frame atoms reduces the frame-to-frame bound to $$\int_{S_q}(1+d(x,z))^{-D-3}(1+d(z,y))^{-D-3}\,dz
 \leq C_D(1+d(x,y))^{-D}.$$ The inequality follows by dividing the integral into the sets where $d(x,z)\geq d(x,y)/2$ and where $d(z,y)\geq d(x,y)/2$. Finally $$|\langle e_j(y),k_z\rangle|^2
 =\binom qj\sin^{2j}(\vartheta/2)\cos^{2(q-j)}(\vartheta/2),$$ where $\vartheta$ is the angle between $y$ and $z$. For bounded $j$, this is bounded by $C_j(1+d(y,z))^{2j}e^{-c d(y,z)^2}$; increasing the constant covers bounded $q$. Inserting the coherent resolution once more proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-locality-common-frames"></a>
<div id="locality:common-frames" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Common frames for nearby fluxes). *For each fixed $d_0$, frames at fluxes $q$ and $t$ with $0\leq q-t\leq d_0$ may be embedded in the same slots so that index identification $i_{q,t}:e_j^{(t)}\mapsto e_j^{(q)}$, $j\leq t$, satisfies the exact identity $\mathsf S_t=\mathsf S_q i_{q,t}$. All preceding estimates remain uniform. In the northern third the windows can be chosen from one system independent of the flux; there one may use the common metric with radial variable $\sqrt{1+m}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-common-frames"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-common-frames</summary>

*Proof.* Use the windows and Fourier lengths at flux $q$ for both spaces, and restrict their index sequences to $0\leq j\leq t$ for the smaller space. Equation <a href="#eq:tight-frame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tight-frame">[eq:tight-frame]</a> still holds on those indices. Only a bounded number of end indices have been removed. The windows meeting them have bounded width, or have rapidly small tails in the estimates in which an endpoint extension occurs. The slot positions for the two sphere metrics differ by a bounded distance; the constants may depend on $d_0$. For all sufficiently large $q$, every window centered in the northern third has its full support below $q/2$, because its width is $O(\sqrt q)$. On these supports the construction uses only $\eta(\sqrt{1+j}-k)$ and their fixed normalization, and their Fourier length is the fixed integer $16(k+1)$. These complete slot vectors are therefore independent of $q$ in index coordinates. The buffer between $q/3$ and $q/2$ permits changing the frame outside this region without changing its northern slots. Bounded fluxes may use pairwise padded frames; no long sequence of common slots is needed there. ◻

</details>

Whenever operators at these two fluxes are subtracted, we first use $i_{q,t}$ and extend the smaller operator by the identity on the added orbital modes. Thus, under the graded decomposition $\mathcal F_q=\mathcal F_t\,\widehat\otimes\,
\bigwedge\operatorname{span}\{e_{t+1},\ldots,e_q\}$, the padded operator is $A\widehat\otimes I$. Extra modes are put in their vacuum only when comparing states or taking the physical compression.

The fermionic Fock space over $\ell^2(\Lambda_q)$ is a graded tensor product of two-dimensional slot spaces. The exterior isometry $\Gamma(\mathsf S_q)$ identifies $\mathcal F_q$ with the subspace in which the orthogonal complement of $\mathsf S_qU_q$ is vacant. A *physical* operator is an operator in the CAR algebra generated by $\mathsf S_qU_q$, extended as the identity on the complementary modes. It preserves that vacant-complement subspace. For $X\subset\Lambda_q$, let $\mathfrak A(X)$ be the CAR algebra of its slots. Put $\mathcal N_{\mathrm{slot}}=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_q}c_x^*c_x$. An operator has number grade $k\in\mathbb Z$ if $[\mathcal N_{\mathrm{slot}},A]=kA$, and parity $\epsilon_A\in\{0,1\}$ if $(-1)^{\mathcal N_{\mathrm{slot}}}A(-1)^{\mathcal N_{\mathrm{slot}}}
=(-1)^{\epsilon_A}A$. Operators of even parity on disjoint sets commute; homogeneous operators obey the graded relation $$[A,B]_{\mathrm{gr}}=AB-(-1)^{\epsilon_A\epsilon_B}BA=0.$$ All Hamiltonians below have even parity. Local expansions can be projected onto any prescribed number grade by averaging the gauge action of the total slot number $\mathcal N_{\mathrm{slot}}$. This action preserves every slot algebra and does not increase approximation errors. On physical operators its grading agrees with that of the physical number operator $\mathcal N$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Local norms and summation

<a id="locality:ball-norm"></a>
<a id="eq:tempered-weight"></a>
For a center $x$, an operator $A$, and $l>0$, define $$\|A\|_{x,l}=\|A\|+
 \sup_{r\geq1}(1+r)^l
       \inf_{A_r\in\mathfrak A(B_r(x))}\|A-A_r\|.
 \label{locality:ball-norm}$$ We call $A$ rapidly local at $x$ if this is finite for every $l$, with bounds uniform in the volume under consideration. An interaction is a family $A_x$ integrated against a measure whose mass in every unit ball is bounded. A bound of *size* $a(x)$ means $\|A_x\|_{x,l}\leq C_l a(x)$ for every $l$. A positive function $a$ is called tempered if, for fixed $C,\kappa$, $$a(x)\leq C a(y)(1+d(x,y))^\kappa.
 \label{eq:tempered-weight}$$ The constants in estimates involving $a$ may depend on $C,\kappa$. Powers and products of $u,v,w$ are examples.

<a id="eq:ball-decomposition"></a>
Taking near-best approximations at radii $2^k$ and subtracting successive ones gives a ball decomposition $$A=\sum_{k\geq0}A^{(k)},\qquad
 A^{(k)}\in\mathfrak A(B_{2^{k+1}}(x)),\qquad
 \|A^{(k)}\|\leq C_l\|A\|_{x,l}2^{-kl}.
 \label{eq:ball-decomposition}$$ Conversely such a decomposition implies the approximation bound, with any slightly smaller exponent. We use this elementary equivalence to pass between operators and interactions on balls.

<a id="eq:local-core-tail"></a>
An observable supported initially on a large ball needs a separate convention. A family has *core radius $r$ and amplitude $a$* if $$\|A\|\leq Ca,\qquad
 \inf_{A_R\in\mathfrak A(B_R(x))}\|A-A_R\|
       \leq C_Da(1+R)^{-D}\quad(R\geq C_*r),
 \label{eq:local-core-tail}$$ where the geometric constant $C_*$ is independent of $D$. The amplitude may contain a specified power of $r$. This convention does not assert that every centered norm <a href="#locality:ball-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="locality:ball-norm">[locality:ball-norm]</a> is bounded by $Ca$ without a further radius factor.

<a id="stmt-locality-core-shells"></a>
<div id="locality:core-shells" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-core-moment"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Shells outside a fixed core). *If <a href="#eq:local-core-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-core-tail">[eq:local-core-tail]</a> holds with $r\geq1$, there is a decomposition $A=\sum_{\nu\geq0}A_\nu$ with $A_\nu\in\mathfrak A(B_{C_*2^{\nu+1}r}(x))$ and $\|A_\nu\|\leq C_Da2^{-\nu D}$ for every $D$. Consequently, for each fixed $B\geq0$, $$\sum_{\nu\geq0}(C_*2^{\nu+1}r)^B\|A_\nu\|
       \leq C_Ba r^B.
 \label{eq:local-core-moment}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-core-shells"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-core-shells</summary>

*Proof.* Take approximants at radii $C_*2^\nu r$ and telescope, starting with the first approximant as the core. The core norm is $Ca$; the other differences are bounded by the two adjoining errors in <a href="#eq:local-core-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-core-tail">[eq:local-core-tail]</a>. Sum with any $D>B+1$ to obtain <a href="#eq:local-core-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-core-moment">[eq:local-core-moment]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-locality-calculus"></a>
<div id="locality:calculus" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Products, commutators, and weighted sums). *The following bounds are uniform over the slot systems above.*

1.  *Products of operators rapidly local at the same center, and adjoints, preserve rapid locality. If $A,B$ are centered at $x,y$, respectively, with bounded local norms, then $\|[A,B]_{\mathrm{gr}}\|\leq C_D(1+d(x,y))^{-D}$ for every $D$.*

2.  *If $A$ is rapidly local at $x$ and a rapidly local interaction $K_y$ has tempered size $a(y)$, then $[\int K_y\,dy,A]_{\mathrm{gr}}$ is rapidly local at $x$, of size $a(x)$ times the local bounds of $A$.*

<a id="eq:commutator-square-locality"></a>
3.  *Suppose $A$ is even, $\|A\|_{x,l}\leq1$, and $J_y$ has bounded rapid local norms. With $D=1+d(x,y)$ and $C_{xy}=[A,J_y]$, $$\|C_{xy}\|\leq C D^{-l},\qquad
     \|C_{xy}\|_{x,l}\leq C,\qquad
     \|C_{xy}^*C_{xy}\|_{x,l}\leq C D^{-l}.
     \label{eq:commutator-square-locality}$$*

*For an operator initially supported in $B_r(x)$, the corresponding core estimates cost a fixed polynomial in $r$. At distances larger than a fixed multiple of $r$, any prescribed decay order is available.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* The product estimate follows from $$\|AB-A_rB_r\|
 \leq\|A-A_r\|\|B\|+\|A_r\|\|B-B_r\|.$$ For the commutator use approximations of radius less than $d(x,y)/3$; their graded commutator vanishes. For the weighted assertion expand both factors as in <a href="#eq:ball-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-decomposition">[eq:ball-decomposition]</a>. Terms with disjoint balls vanish, while intersecting balls of radii $R,S$ require $d(x,y)\leq R+S$. The number or measure of their possible centers is $O((1+R+S)^2)$, and <a href="#eq:tempered-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tempered-weight">[eq:tempered-weight]</a> costs at most $C(1+R+S)^\kappa$. To obtain an output moment of order $D$, bound these costs by $C(1+R+S)^{D+\kappa+2}$ and choose input decay order larger than $D+\kappa+6$. The two geometric sums then converge. This argument also permits assigning each output term to the center of either input; that convention will be used for differences of interactions.

For <a href="#eq:commutator-square-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator-square-locality">[eq:commutator-square-locality]</a>, the first estimate follows by the disjoint-ball argument at exponent $l$. For approximation radii $r\leq3D$, use zero as an approximant to $C_{xy}$, giving $r^l\|C_{xy}\|\leq C$. For $r>3D$, approximate $A$ in $B_{r/3}(x)$ and $J_y$ in $B_{r/3}(y)$; their commutator is supported in $B_r(x)$ and has error $Cr^{-l}$. Thus the second estimate holds. Apply the product estimate with the small norm $\|C_{xy}\|\leq CD^{-l}$ on one factor to obtain the third estimate. Finally a ball $B_r(x)$ contains $O((1+r)^2)$ slots, and tempered ratios within it cost $O((1+r)^\kappa)$. The same sums give the stated polynomial core costs. For tails beyond $Cr$, the distance from the original ball is comparable to the tail radius, so increasing a tail order does not increase the core-volume exponent. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-locality-toeplitz"></a>
<div id="locality:toeplitz" class="corollary">

**Corollary 8** (The physical interactions). *<span id="locality:basic-interactions" label="locality:basic-interactions"></span> The unperturbed Hamiltonian $H_q$ and the Fock lift of any bounded real scalar symbol $\varphi$ have physical, neutral, Hermitian rapid interactions of uniform sizes $C_D$ and $C_D\|\varphi\|_\infty$, respectively.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-toeplitz"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-toeplitz</summary>

*Proof.* Coefficient localization gives operator-norm localization directly. If $E_R$ is the one-particle projection onto slots in $B_R(x)$, the CAR identity $\|c(f)\|=\|f\|_2$ gives $$\|c(\mathsf S_q f)-c(E_R\mathsf S_q f)\|
       =\|(I-E_R)\mathsf S_q f\|_2.$$ For $f=k_x$ or a bounded polar mode at $x$, Lemma <a href="#locality:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:frame">4</a> and the slot count <a href="#eq:slot-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slot-volume">[eq:slot-volume]</a> make the right side at most $C_D(1+R)^{-D}$ for every $D$; the approximating annihilator is supported in $B_R(x)$.

The coherent resolution gives $$d\Gamma(T_q(\varphi))=\frac{q+1}{2\pi q}
       \int_{S_q}\varphi(x)c^*(k_x)c(k_x)\,dA(x).$$ For $H_q$, the highest pair vector is $v_0=e_0\wedge e_1$. Schur’s Lemma on the spin-$(q-1)$ pair space and its dimension $2q-1$ give, with Haar mass one, <a id="eq:coherent-pair-resolution"></a>$$H_q=(2q-1)\int_{SU(2)}B(gv_0)^*B(gv_0)\,dg.
 \label{eq:coherent-pair-resolution}$$ The product $B(gv_0)=c(ge_1)c(ge_0)$ is rapidly local at the rotated pole by Lemmas <a href="#locality:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:frame">4</a> and <a href="#locality:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:calculus">7</a>. Its phase along the stabilizer disappears in its square, so the center measure on the sphere has density $(2q-1)/(2\pi q)$. This and $(q+1)/(2\pi q)$ are uniformly bounded. The same two lemmas localize $c^*(k_x)c(k_x)$. No derivative of $\varphi$ enters either the representation or its bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Propagation and spectral filters

We next establish that a time filter preserves this local calculus. The relevant propagation bound is needed only with arbitrary polynomial decay, which permits a direct truncation argument. The underlying commutator iteration is the Lieb–Robinson method <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[LR](#ref-LR)], in its graded fermionic form <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[NSY](#ref-NSY)]; its use with time filters is the locality mechanism behind quasi-adiabatic continuation and spectral flow <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[HW](#ref-HW), [BMNS](#ref-BMNS)].

<a id="stmt-locality-dynamics"></a>
<div id="locality:dynamics" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:polynomial-propagation"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Propagation of local observables). *Let $K$ be self-adjoint and even, with a rapidly local interaction of uniform size. Write $\tau_t^K(A)=e^{itK}Ae^{-itK}$. For each $D$ there are $D'$ and $M_D$, independent of volume, such that $$\|\tau_t^K(A)\|_{x,D}
       \leq C_D(1+|t|)^{M_D}\|A\|_{x,D'}.
 \label{eq:polynomial-propagation}$$ The assertion holds also for time-dependent generators with the same uniform bounds. In particular evolution over a bounded parameter interval preserves rapid locality. If $A$ is supported in $B_r(x)$, the error in approximating $\tau_t^K(A)$ on $B_R(x)$, for $R\geq C(r+(1+|t|)^4)$, is bounded by $C_D\|A\|(1+r)^2R^{-D}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-dynamics"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-dynamics</summary>

<a id="eq:truncated-LR"></a>
*Proof.* Use <a href="#eq:ball-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-decomposition">[eq:ball-decomposition]</a> to write $K$ as a sum of terms on balls. Taking Hermitian and even parts makes each term Hermitian and even without changing the total or its estimates. First retain only terms of diameter at most $\ell$. For this truncated interaction define $$M_{ab}=2\sum_{X\ni a,b}\|K_X\|.$$ Uniform summability, including the number of sites in $X$, gives $\sup_a\sum_b M_{ab}e^{d(a,b)/\ell}\leq C$; indeed each retained distance is at most $\ell$, and the ball-volume costs are polynomial. The iterated commutator inequality, with internal evolution removed unitarily at each step, therefore gives $$\|[\tau_t^{K_{\leq\ell}}(A),B]_{\mathrm{gr}}\|
 \leq C\|A\|\|B\|\,|X|\,|Y|
        e^{C|t|-d(X,Y)/\ell}
 \label{eq:truncated-LR}$$ for $A\in\mathfrak A(X)$ and $B\in\mathfrak A(Y)$. For completeness, the $n$th iterated integral contributes $|t|^n/n!$ times a chain of $n$ matrix factors $M$. Multiplication by $e^{d(X,Y)/\ell}$ is bounded by the product of the exponential weights along the chain. Summing the intermediate sites gives $C^n$, and summing $n$ gives <a href="#eq:truncated-LR" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-LR">[eq:truncated-LR]</a>. Evenness of the generator makes the same calculation valid for odd $A,B$ with the graded commutator.

<a id="eq:range-restoration"></a>
Restore the removed terms using Duhamel’s formula. For each removed ball term use the smaller of the trivial commutator bound and <a href="#eq:truncated-LR" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-LR">[eq:truncated-LR]</a>. The centers within distance $C\ell(1+|t|)$ of $X$, together with the radius of that term, occupy at most a polynomial volume. Outside that region the exponential bound is summable. Consequently, for every $s$, the restoration error is at most $$C_s\|A\||X|(1+|t|)
       (1+r+\ell(1+|t|))^4\ell^{-s},
 \label{eq:range-restoration}$$ when $X\subset B_r(x)$. To see the power allowance explicitly, a removed term of radius $a$ contributes its norm times at most $C(1+r+\ell(1+|t|)+a)^2$ possible centers and at most $C(1+a)^2$ sites. Its arbitrarily high radius moment makes the sum over $a>\ell/2$ bounded by the right side of <a href="#eq:range-restoration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:range-restoration">[eq:range-restoration]</a>, after increasing the input moment by $s+6$.

Now let $R\geq C(r+(1+|t|)^4)$ and choose $\ell=R^{1/3}$. The truncated evolution differs from the evolution generated by its terms contained in $B_R(x)$ by $C\|A\||X|\operatorname{poly}(R)e^{C|t|-cR^{2/3}}$. This is again Duhamel’s formula and <a href="#eq:truncated-LR" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-LR">[eq:truncated-LR]</a>, summed over the boundary terms. Both restoration errors are bounded by $C_D\|A\||X|R^{-D}$, choosing $s$ sufficiently large in <a href="#eq:range-restoration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:range-restoration">[eq:range-restoration]</a>. This supplies an approximation in $\mathfrak A(B_R(x))$. At smaller radii use the norm bound $\|\tau_t(A)\|=\|A\|$. Since $|X|\leq C(1+r)^2$, this proves the assertion for supported $A$ and then, by <a href="#eq:ball-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-decomposition">[eq:ball-decomposition]</a>, <a href="#eq:polynomial-propagation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-propagation">[eq:polynomial-propagation]</a>. The proof uses only uniform bounds on the interaction at each time, so also proves the time-dependent assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-locality-filters"></a>
<div id="locality:filters" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Time filters). *Suppose $F\in L^1(\mathbb R)$ and $\int(1+|t|)^j|F(t)|\,dt<\infty$ for every $j$. Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#locality:dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:dynamics">9</a>, <a id="eq:time-filter"></a>$$\mathcal T_{F,K}(A)=\int_{\mathbb R}F(t)\tau_t^K(A)\,dt
 \label{eq:time-filter}$$ is rapidly local whenever $A$ is, with uniform bounds. The same is true for a finite measure consisting of such a function and a mass at zero. Number grade and physicality are preserved when $K$ is neutral and physical. Suitable symmetry of $F$ preserves adjoints.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-locality-filters"><summary>Proof of stmt-locality-filters</summary>

*Proof.* Integrate <a href="#eq:polynomial-propagation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-propagation">[eq:polynomial-propagation]</a>. The approximation norm is a seminorm up to its operator-norm summand, so the integral of local approximants gives the required estimate. The remaining assertions follow directly by commuting the evolution with gauge transformations, the physical algebra, and adjoints. ◻

</details>

The frequency functions used below are smooth, vanish on an interval about zero, and have tails proportional to $1/\omega$ on one or both half-lines. Their inverse Fourier transforms satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#locality:filters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:filters">10</a>. Here is a convenient verification. Every positive-order derivative of such a multiplier is integrable, so integration by parts gives arbitrary powers of decay for $|t|\geq1$. For $0<|t|<1$, split the frequency integral at $|\omega|=|t|^{-1}$. The inner portion is $O(1+|\log|t||)$, and one integration by parts on the outer portion is $O(1)$. Thus the possible singularity at zero is integrable. A smooth compactly supported multiplier has a Schwartz kernel. These facts cover both spectral transport and the filters that retain only small energy differences.

<a id="stmt-locality-difference"></a>
<div id="locality:difference" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:difference-core"></a>
<a id="eq:difference-tail"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Weighted differences). *Let $K,K'$ satisfy Lemma <a href="#locality:dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:dynamics">9</a> uniformly, and assume $K-K'$ has a rapidly local interaction of tempered size $a(z)$. For a rapidly local $A$ centered at $x$, <a id="eq:weighted-evolution-difference"></a>$$\|\tau_t^K(A)-\tau_t^{K'}(A)\|_{x,D}
 \leq C_Da(x)(1+|t|)^{M_D}\|A\|_{x,D'}.
 \label{eq:weighted-evolution-difference}$$ The same conclusion holds after an all-moments time filter, and for differences of bounded-parameter evolutions.
