     \mathcal D_{q^0,3}(O)+R_{q^0,E}(O)\bigr).$$ Thus the parent-to-child reference change is determined by a filled step. Define $$\begin{split}
 R_{q,E}'(O)&=R_{q,E}(O)
           -\operatorname{ref}_{t^0}(R_{q^0,E}(O))I,\\
 s_q(O)&=\operatorname{ref}_t(\mathcal D_{q,3}(O))
         -\operatorname{ref}_{t^0}(\mathcal D_{q^0,3}(O)).
 \end{split}$$ The references in the second line use the same filled state. Lemma <a href="#lem:flags-reference-compatibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flags-reference-compatibility">25</a>, first on common northern slots and then on rapid tails by norm approximation, therefore gives $$s_q(O)=\operatorname{ref}_{t^0}\bigl(
     \mathcal D_{q,3}(O)-\mathcal D_{q^0,3}(O)\bigr)
     +O(q^{-D}).$$ The bounded-flux comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:flags-northern-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flags-northern-step">24</a> supplies the additional factor $q^{-1}$. Substituting these scalar identities into the branch comparison yields $$\begin{split}
 J_{q,E}^*(O-\operatorname{ref}_q(O))J_{q,E}
 ={}&(O-\operatorname{ref}_t(O))
    +\bigl(\mathcal D_{q,3}(O)
               -\operatorname{ref}_t(\mathcal D_{q,3}(O))\bigr)\\
    &+s_q(O)I+R_{q,E}'(O),\\
 |s_q(O)|\leq{}&Cr^c u/q^3+C_Dq^{-D},\qquad
 \|R_{q,E}'(O)\|\leq C_Dq^{-D}.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:flags-centered-E}$$ Here and below the symbols denote compressions to the relevant child zero space. When $h=0$ the comparison is its own filled calibration, so all centered contributions vanish exactly.

<a id="eq:flags-centered-F"></a>
For an $F$ step, $t=q-1$. The nearest smaller filled fluxes are either identical or differ by three. In the latter case apply the filled $E$ comparison just used. Together with <a href="#eq:flags-northern-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-northern-size">[eq:flags-northern-size]</a>, this proves $$J_{q,F}^*(O-\operatorname{ref}_q(O))J_{q,F}
 =O-\operatorname{ref}_t(O)+S_{q,F}(O),\qquad
 \|S_{q,F}(O)\|\leq Cr^cu/q^2+C_Dq^{-D}.
 \label{eq:flags-centered-F}$$ The off-diagonal blocks have norm $C_Dq^{-D}$. An $F$ step already records a flag, so its entire error will be charged to that flag.

Here is a uniform closure argument, including the parameter choices. Let $c,b_0$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:flags-northern-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flags-northern-step">24</a>. Choose $b>\max\{b_0,c\}$, then choose $B>\max\{ab,c\}$ large enough to absorb the fixed polynomial costs of the local calculus. A constant $M$ will be fixed after $B$. Set $$\tau=Mr^b u.$$ If $q\leq\tau$ and $h>0$, every flag has $b'\leq q$, so $$W_m\geq(2Mr^b)^{-a}I.$$ The norm bound $\|O-\operatorname{ref}_q(O)\|\leq2$ proves <a href="#eq:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-local-readout">[eq:flags-local-readout]</a> in this region with constant $2(2M)^a$. When $h=0$ the centered compression is zero. Increasing $M$ includes all stopping cases in this argument.

<a id="eq:flags-bootstrap-constant"></a>
Fix a finite $Q$, and let $K_Q$ be the least constant in <a href="#eq:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-local-readout">[eq:flags-local-readout]</a> for fluxes at most $Q$, with the chosen $B$, all number sectors, and all allowed frames. This number is finite: for $h>0$ use $W_m\geq(1+Q)^{-a}$, while the filled compression is exactly centered. We prove $$K_Q\leq C_M+\frac12K_Q
 \label{eq:flags-bootstrap-constant}$$ with $C_M$ independent of $Q$.

Suppose $q>\tau$. Expand the unchanged $O$ down the branching tree, using <a href="#eq:flags-centered-E" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-centered-E">[eq:flags-centered-E]</a> and <a href="#eq:flags-centered-F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-centered-F">[eq:flags-centered-F]</a>, until the current flux is at most $\tau$. The original northern slots, center, and radius are unchanged through this part of the descent. The terminal comparisons have just been bounded. In an $E$ child, decompose $\mathcal D_{q,3}(O)$ into its core and dyadic ball shells, all centered at $m$. Equation <a href="#eq:pinning-northern-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pinning-northern-moment">[eq:pinning-northern-moment]</a>, with a tail order larger than $B+2$, gives $$\sum_\nu r_\nu^B\|\mathcal D_{q,3}(O)_\nu\|
 \leq C(B)r^{B+c}u/q^2.$$ Applying the definition of $K_Q$ to each smaller-flux shell, and using linearity of the reference, bounds its centered contribution by $$K_QC(B)r^{B+c}u/q^2$$ times the remaining history weight in that child. Along any word, the flux strictly decreases; thus $$\sum_{q'>\tau}\frac{u}{(q')^2}\leq C\frac{u}{\tau}
       =\frac{C}{Mr^b}.$$ Consequently all induction-dependent contributions are bounded by $$K_Q\frac{C(B)}M r^{B+c-b}W_m.$$ Choose $M$ so large that this is at most $\tfrac12 K_Qr^BW_m$. This choice is possible because $b>c$ and $r\geq2$.

We verify that the other errors have a bound independent of $K_Q$. At a node with positive remaining hole number, its identity is bounded by the sum of the identities assigned to its remaining flags. A scalar electron error may therefore be charged to every such flag. For a flag at $\ell$, the total charge from ancestors in <a href="#eq:flags-centered-E" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-centered-E">[eq:flags-centered-E]</a> is at most <a id="eq:flags-scalar-tail"></a>$$C\frac{r^cu}{\max\{\tau,1+\ell\}^2}.
 \label{eq:flags-scalar-tail}$$ The $F$ error in <a href="#eq:flags-centered-F" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-centered-F">[eq:flags-centered-F]</a> occurs at its own flag and obeys the same bound, up to a fixed constant. Write $w_\ell=\min\{1,(u/(1+\ell))^a\}$. Since $a<2$ and $\tau=Mr^bu$, $$\frac{r^cu}{\max\{\tau,1+\ell\}^2}
 \leq C M^{a-2}r^{c+b(a-2)}w_\ell
 \leq C_M r^B w_\ell.$$ The $q^{-D}$ remainders, including the off-diagonal blocks, obey the same estimate after choosing $D>a+2$. For a self-adjoint off-diagonal block, its norm bounds the full block matrix above and below by that norm times the identity at its node, so the same charge applies. Nodes with $h=0$ contribute nothing, by the exact centered filled identity. Summation of these diagonal bounds along words proves <a href="#eq:flags-bootstrap-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-bootstrap-constant">[eq:flags-bootstrap-constant]</a>.

It follows that $K_Q\leq2C_M$ for every $Q$, proving the uniform claim. Finally, the assertion for $A=\sum_\nu A_\nu$ follows by linearity of the reference and by summing the form inequalities. ◻

</details>

The theorem estimates a compression to the zero spaces. It does not claim that ordinary local fluctuations vanish in the filled state. It also applies to coherent superpositions of histories: all charges above are diagonal operator bounds, rather than bounds conditional on a classical choice of word.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Rotation averaging in a one-hole space

The history estimate still depends on a chosen readout axis. In this subsection, a one-hole space means a space with quasihole degree $h=1$. Representation theory then removes the axis dependence and yields an integrated estimate with no factor of the sphere area.

<a id="stmt-cor-flags-one-hole"></a>
<div id="cor:flags-one-hole" class="corollary">

**Corollary 27** (One-hole comparison). *Suppose $h=1$, equivalently $q=3n-2$. Let $\psi,\phi$ be any two unit vectors in $Z_{n,q}$. Let $\{O_x\}$ be a measurable family of neutral self-adjoint observables of norm at most one, supported in balls of a fixed radius $r\geq2$ about $x$, with center measure of bounded density. There are constants $C,B'<\infty$, independent of $n,q$, such that <a id="eq:flags-one-hole"></a>$$\int\left|
 \langle\psi,O_x\psi\rangle-\langle\phi,O_x\phi\rangle
 \right|\,dx\leq Cr^{B'}.
 \label{eq:flags-one-hole}$$ The same conclusion holds for rapidly localized families, with the right side bounded by the corresponding fixed rapid-locality seminorm. For a family whose norms are bounded by $A$, multiply the right side by $A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-flags-one-hole"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-flags-one-hole</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $a\in(1,2)$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a>. The symmetric-polynomial factor in <a href="#eq:flags-polynomial-space" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-polynomial-space">[eq:flags-polynomial-space]</a> has degree one in each variable. It is the irreducible spin-$n/2$ representation, so $Z_{n,q}$ is irreducible and has dimension $n+1$.

When $h=1$, an $F$ branch has $h=0$ and hence dimension one. Before that branch all steps are $E$, which lower the flux by three. Therefore at most one one-dimensional history has a flag at any specified nonterminal flux. The history that reaches the stopping region without an $F$ step has bounded terminal dimension. It follows that, as an operator on $Z_{n,q}$, <a id="eq:flags-one-hole-trace"></a>$$\operatorname{Tr}(W_0)
 \leq C+\sum_{b\geq0}(1+b)^{-a}\leq C_a.
 \label{eq:flags-one-hole-trace}$$ The trace is unchanged on conjugating back to the physical zero space. Write $\widehat W_0=\mathcal R_{n,q}^*W_0\mathcal R_{n,q}$ for that physical operator.

Let $V_q(g)$ denote the rotation representation, with normalized Haar measure $dg$. Schur’s lemma gives $$\int_{\mathrm{SU}(2)}V_q(g)\widehat W_0V_q(g)^*\,dg
 =\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(\widehat W_0)}{n+1}I.$$ For each rotation use the readout axis whose north pole is the image of the original north pole. Precisely, write $x(g)=g\cdot\mathrm{north}$ and $W^{g}=V_q(g)\widehat W_0V_q(g)^*$. Use the rotated frame and rotated branch construction at $x(g)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a>, applied to $O_{x(g)}$, gives $$\bigl|\langle\psi,O_{x(g)}\psi\rangle
       -\langle\phi,O_{x(g)}\phi\rangle\bigr|
 \leq Cr^{B'}\bigl(\langle\psi,W^g\psi\rangle
                    +\langle\phi,W^g\phi\rangle\bigr).$$ The two reference expectations cancel in this inequality. Changes between local frames cost only a fixed polynomial in $r$ by the local calculus. Integrating rotations and multiplying by the sphere area yields at most $Cr^{B'}q\operatorname{Tr}(W_0)/(n+1)$, which is uniformly bounded because $q=3n-2$. For any center measure with bounded mass in unit balls, spread the mass at each center over a ball of fixed radius. The resulting density relative to area is bounded uniformly. The operator originally assigned to $x$ is supported in a ball of radius $r+1$ about every point in that spreading ball, so the same pointwise bound applies before integration. This reduces the general center measure to the area estimate. Finally, summing ball shells proves the assertion for rapid families. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:charge"></a>
### Charge bounds and rotation averaging

The history estimate of Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a> controls a local observable by weighted flags. To treat arbitrary hole number by rotation averaging, we compare these weights with a physical one-body observable, first with a slowly decreasing weight and then with a summable weight.

Increase the fixed stopping flux in the history construction, if necessary, so that it is at least eight and all pinning comparisons above it are valid, including shifts by at most three. Write $q_{\rm s}=q_{\mathrm{stop}}$ and let $\mathcal R_{n,q}$ denote the history unitary in <a href="#eq:flags-history-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flags-history-map">[eq:flags-history-map]</a>. For $L\ge2$ and $0<p<2$, put <a id="eq:charge-definitions"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-definitions}
 \begin{gathered}
 f(x)=f_{L,p}(x)=\left(\frac{L}{L+x}\right)^p,\qquad
 C_q(f)=\frac{q+3}{3(q+1)}\sum_{j=0}^q f(j),\\
 S_{L,p}(\omega)=\sum_{b\in\mathcal B(\omega)}f(b).
 \end{gathered}$$ When $q$ is divisible by three, $C_q(f)$ is the filled-state expectation of $D_q(f)$: rotational invariance gives occupation $(q+3)/(3(q+1))$ in every orbital. The formula defines the scalar $C_q(f)$ also at the other fluxes. The operator $S_{L,p}$ is diagonal in histories and scalar on each terminal summand. Its sum is over the flag multiset $\mathcal B(\omega)$, including all terminal flags at position zero; each history has exactly $h=q+3-3n$ flags. All operator inequalities involving $S_{L,p}$ below are in history coordinates. In particular, they make no measurement assumption about flags in a zero-mode vector.

<a id="stmt-prop-charge-pooled"></a>
<div id="prop:charge-pooled" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:charge-pooled"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Pooled charge). *For each of $p=1/8$ and $p=3/2$ there are fixed $L\ge2$ and $c>0$ such that, for every flux and every nonzero zero-mode space $Z_{n,q}$, $$\label{eq:charge-pooled}
 \mathcal R_{n,q}\bigl(C_q(f)\mathbf1-P_{n,q}D_q(f)P_{n,q}\bigr)\mathcal R_{n,q}^*
 \ \ge\ c S_{L,p}.$$ Here compression to $Z_{n,q}$ is understood, and $P_{n,q}$ denotes its orthogonal projection. The constants are independent of $q,n$ and of the readout axis. When $h=0$, both sides vanish.*

</div>

The obstruction to this estimate occurs at electron steps. Along a history, each F step contributes approximately $-f(q)/3$ to $D_q(f)-C_q(f)\mathbf1$, supplying a credit for its new flag. Each E step contributes a positive scalar correction. When at least two flags remain, divide this scalar cost between them. The scalar tail estimate below leaves the strictly positive margin $$\frac13-\frac{1+p}{6(1-p)}=\frac5{42}\qquad(p=1/8).$$ With one remaining flag, there is no such margin. We instead combine the scalar with the transported observable and compare it to a filled step; the one-hole comparison of Corollary <a href="#cor:flags-one-hole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:flags-one-hole">27</a> makes the resulting error summably small. These two cases prove the first charge estimate.

Rotation averaging then bounds integrated local-expectation differences between two zero-mode states by $C(q+1)^{7/8}$ times the sum of their hole numbers. This still grows with the area, but it is enough to prove the charge estimate again with $p=3/2$. For a polar annulus $1+m\asymp M$, use this averaged bound while the flux is moderate relative to $M$; the transport interaction has a factor $(q+1)^{-2}$ that pays for the area dependence. At larger flux, apply the original fixed-axis history estimate directly to the annulus. The two resulting sums are small when the new weight scale $L$ is large. The summable weight then gives the area-independent local-expectation bound at the end of this section.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Scalar telescoping and summable error bounds

We estimate $D_q(f)-C_q(f)\mathbf1$ from above. An F step contributes the scalar $C_{q-1}(f)-C_q(f)$, and an E step contributes <a id="eq:charge-electron-scalar"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-electron-scalar}
 \ell_q=C_{q-3}(f)+f(q)-C_q(f).$$ For the continuous comparison define <a id="eq:charge-continuous-scalar"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-continuous-scalar}
 F(x)=\frac1x\int_0^x f(t)\,dt,\qquad
 \ell(x)=-2F'(x)+f'(x)\quad(x>0),$$ with the continuous extensions at zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-charge-scalars"></a>
<div id="lem:charge-scalars" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:charge-F-scalar"></a>
<a id="eq:charge-scalar-tail"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Scalar estimates). *Fix $p\in(0,2)$ and a lower stopping flux $q_{\rm s}\ge8$. There is a function $\varepsilon_p(L)\to0$ as $L\to\infty$ with the following properties, uniformly for $q\ge q_{\rm s}$: <a id="eq:charge-calibration-scalar"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \left|C_{q-1}(f)-C_q(f)+\frac13f(q)\right|
 &\le\varepsilon_p(L)f(q),\label{eq:charge-F-scalar}\\
 \sum_{q\ge q_{\rm s}}
 \frac{|\ell_q-\ell_{3\lfloor q/3\rfloor}|}{f(q)}
 &\le\varepsilon_p(L).\label{eq:charge-calibration-scalar}
\end{aligned}$$ The function $\ell$ is nonnegative. If $0<p<1$, then for every collection $\mathcal E$ of E-step fluxes along a history, and every flag position $k$ below these steps, $$\label{eq:charge-scalar-tail}
 \sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal E\\q\ge k}}(\ell_q)_+
 \le \left(\frac{1+p}{3(1-p)}+\varepsilon_p(L)\right)f(k).$$ Changing $k$ by a fixed bounded amount, or taking $k$ in the terminal range, only changes $\varepsilon_p(L)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-charge-scalars"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-charge-scalars</summary>

<a id="eq:charge-positive-scalar"></a>
*Proof.* The exact formula <a id="eq:charge-scalar-exact"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-scalar-exact}
 \ell_q=f(q)-\left(1+\frac2{q+1}\right)
 \frac{f(q)+f(q-1)+f(q-2)}3
 +\frac{2\sum_{j=0}^{q-3}f(j)}{(q+1)(q-2)}$$ is obtained by separating the last three summands in $C_q$. A useful positive form of this identity is obtained by putting $d_j=f(j)-2f(j+1)+f(j+2)$ and $Q_q=(q+1)(q-2)$: $$\label{eq:charge-positive-scalar}
 \ell_q=\frac1{Q_q}\sum_{j=0}^{q-3}(j+1)(j+2)d_j
       +\frac{2q}{3(q+1)}d_{q-2}.$$ Expanding the second differences verifies the formula coefficient by coefficient. In particular, $\ell_q\ge0$, and constant and affine functions contribute zero.

<a id="eq:charge-scalar-errors"></a>
<a id="eq:charge-scalar-differences"></a>
Here are precise discrete-to-continuous error bounds: $$\begin{aligned}
 |\ell_q-\ell(q)|+
 \sup_{x\in[q-3,q]}|\ell(x)-\ell(q)|
 &\le C(L+q)^{-2},\label{eq:charge-scalar-errors}\\
 |\ell_q-\ell_{q-r}|
 &\le
 \begin{cases}
 C L^{-2},&q\le L,\\
 C(F(q)+f(q))q^{-2},&q>L,
 \end{cases}\label{eq:charge-scalar-differences}
\end{aligned}$$ for $r=0,1,2$. We give details because the sharper second estimate is needed for the summable weight. The identity $d_j=\int (1-|t-j-1|)_+f''(t)\,dt$ expresses <a href="#eq:charge-positive-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-positive-scalar">[eq:charge-positive-scalar]</a> as $\int_0^q \kappa_q(t)f''(t)\,dt$. The function $\kappa_q$ is linear between consecutive integers and has values $$\kappa_q(i)=\frac{i(i+1)}{Q_q}\ (0\le i\le q-2),\qquad
 \kappa_q(q-1)=\frac{2q}{3(q+1)},\qquad \kappa_q(q)=0.$$ On $[0,q-2]$ its difference from $t^2/q^2$ is at most $C(t/q^2+t^2/q^3)$, and on $[q-2,q]$ that difference is bounded by a constant. The error in its integral against $f''$ is consequently at most $$C\left(q^{-2}\int_0^q tf''(t)\,dt+
 q^{-3}\int_0^q t^2f''(t)\,dt+
 \int_{q-2}^q f''(t)\,dt\right).$$ For $q\le L$ use $f''\le C/L^2$; for $q>L$ use $\int_0^q tf''\le1$, $\int_0^q t^2f''\le2qF(q)$, and $f''(t)\le C f(t)(L+t)^{-2}$. This proves the first term of <a href="#eq:charge-scalar-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-scalar-errors">[eq:charge-scalar-errors]</a>. The continuous identity $\ell(x)=x^{-2}\int_0^x t^2f''(t)\,dt$ implies $\ell'(x)=f''(x)-2\ell(x)/x$, which proves the oscillation estimate.

For <a href="#eq:charge-scalar-differences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-scalar-differences">[eq:charge-scalar-differences]</a>, difference the positive formula directly: $$\begin{split}
 \ell_q-\ell_{q-1}
 ={}&-\frac{2(q-1)}{Q_qQ_{q-1}}
      \sum_{j=0}^{q-4}(j+1)(j+2)d_j\\
 &+\frac{(q-1)(q-2)}{3q(q+1)}d_{q-3}
  +\frac{2q}{3(q+1)}d_{q-2}.
\end{split}$$ The first sum is bounded by $CqF(q)$, as follows by its integral representation and $\int_0^q t^2f''\le2qF(q)$. This gives $CF(q)/q^2$ when $q>L$; the last two terms cost $Cf(q)/q^2$. Below $L$ use $d_j\le C/L^2$ in the displayed formula. A bounded number of differences proves the claimed bound.

<a id="eq:charge-average-weight"></a>
The exact F-step identity is $$C_{q-1}(f)-C_q(f)+\frac13 f(q)
 =\frac{2}{3q(q+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\bigl(f(j)-f(q)\bigr).$$ For $q\le L$ its ratio to $f(q)$ is $O(L^{-1})$. For $q>L$ use $$\label{eq:charge-average-weight}
 F(q)\le
 \begin{cases}
 f(q)/(1-p),&p<1,\\
 C_pL/q,&p>1.
 \end{cases}$$ The resulting ratio is $O(L^{-1})$ also when $1<p<2$, since $L^{1-p}q^{p-2}\le L^{-1}$ for $q\ge L$. The case $p=1$ follows from the explicit logarithmic integral and is not needed below. Dividing <a href="#eq:charge-scalar-differences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-scalar-differences">[eq:charge-scalar-differences]</a> by $f(q)$ and summing gives $O(L^{-1})$ below $L$. Above $L$ it gives $O(L^{-1})$: the summands are $O(q^{-2})$ for $p<1$ and $O(q^{-2}+L^{1-p}q^{p-3})$ for $1<p<2$.

Twice integrating $f''$ proves $$\ell(x)=\frac1{x^2}\int_0^x t^2f''(t)\,dt\ge0,
 \qquad
 \int_k^\infty\ell(x)\,dx=2F(k)-f(k).$$ The E-step intervals $[q-3,q]$ have disjoint interiors along a history. Consequently their continuous contribution is at most $\frac13\int_{(k-3)_+}^{\infty}\ell(x)\,dx$. For $p<1$, <a href="#eq:charge-average-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-average-weight">[eq:charge-average-weight]</a> bounds this by $\frac{1+p}{3(1-p)}f(k)$, up to $O(L^{-1})f(k)$ from the bounded endpoint shift. Finally, the tail sum of <a href="#eq:charge-scalar-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-scalar-errors">[eq:charge-scalar-errors]</a> is bounded by $C/(L+k)$, and $$\frac1{L+k}\le\frac1L f(k)\qquad(0<p<1).$$ Thus it is $O(L^{-1})f(k)$ uniformly in $k$. This proves <a href="#eq:charge-scalar-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-scalar-tail">[eq:charge-scalar-tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

We next list the analytic error sums that will be used in both charge arguments. Retain the size function $A_q$ from Proposition <a href="#pinning:weighted-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinning:weighted-transport">18</a>. With $u=1+m$, $v=1+q-m$, and $w=\sqrt{uv/(q+1)}$, it satisfies <a id="eq:charge-size"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-size}
 \begin{gathered}
 A_q(m)=\frac{u^2f''(m)}{(q+1)^2}
 \asymp\frac{f(m)}{(q+1)^2}\left(\frac{u}{L+u}\right)^2,\\
 B_q=\int_0^q A_q(m)\,dm,\qquad
 J_q=\int_0^q\frac{A_q(m)}v\,dm.
 \end{gathered}$$ The longitude integral has bounded mass and is included in constants. Proposition <a href="#pinning:weighted-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinning:weighted-transport">18</a> gives this size for each transported change in $D(f)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-charge-sums"></a>
<div id="lem:charge-sums" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:charge-common-sums"></a>
<a id="eq:charge-first-sums"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Uniform error sums). *For either $p=1/8$ or $p=3/2$, as $L\to\infty$, $$\label{eq:charge-common-sums}
 \sup_{q\ge q_{\rm s}}\frac{B_q}{f(q)}=O(L^{-1}),\qquad
 \sum_{q\ge q_{\rm s}}
 \left(\frac{J_q}{f(q)}+\frac1{(L+q)^2}\right)
 =O\left(\frac{1+\log L}{L}\right).$$ For $p=1/8$ there are, in addition, $$\label{eq:charge-first-sums}
 \sum_{q\ge q_{\rm s}}
 \frac{\sup_{0\le m\le q}A_q(m)}{f(q)}=O(L^{-1}),\qquad
 \sum_{q\ge q_{\rm s}}\frac1{f(q)}
 \int_0^q\frac{A_q(m)}w\,dm=O(L^{-1/2}).$$ All implied constants are independent of $L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-charge-sums"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-charge-sums</summary>

*Proof.* For $q\le L$, integrating $u^2/L^2$ gives $B_q\le C(q+1)L^{-2}$; for $q>L$, dropping the factor $(u/(L+u))^2$ gives $B_q\le C F(q)/q$. These estimates and <a href="#eq:charge-average-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-average-weight">[eq:charge-average-weight]</a> prove the first bound in <a href="#eq:charge-common-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-common-sums">[eq:charge-common-sums]</a>. On the northern half of the sphere $v\ge(q+1)/2$, whereas on the southern half $A_q(m)\le C f(q)(L+q)^{-2}$. Therefore $$\frac{J_q}{f(q)}\le
 \frac{C B_q}{(q+1)f(q)}+
 \frac{C\log(q+2)}{(L+q)^2}.$$ The first term sums to $O(L^{-1})$: above $L$ it is bounded by $Cq^{-2}$ for $p<1$ and by $CL^{1-p}q^{p-3}$ for $p>1$. The second sums to $O((1+\log L)/L)$.

For the supremum in <a href="#eq:charge-first-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-first-sums">[eq:charge-first-sums]</a>, use $CL^{-2}$ when $q\le L$ and $C(q+1)^{-2}$ otherwise. The latter divided by $f(q)$ is $O(L^{-p}q^{p-2})$, whose sum is $O(L^{-1})$ for $p<1$. On the northern half, $w\ge c\sqrt u$, so the last integral is at most $$\frac{C}{(q+1)^2}\int_0^{q/2}
 \frac{f(m)u^{3/2}}{(L+u)^2}\,dm
 +\frac{C\sqrt{q+1}\,f(q)}{(L+q)^2}.$$ After division by $f(q)$, both terms are bounded by $C\sqrt{q+1}/L^2$ for $q\le L$ and by $Cq^{-3/2}$ for $q>L$, provided $p<1/2$. Their sums are $O(L^{-1/2})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Calibration and the first charge estimate

Set $K_q:=K_{q,3}(f)$ on $\mathcal F_{q-3}$. Its compression to $Z_{n-1,q-3}$ is the transported change in an E step from $Z_{n,q}$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:flags-weighted-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flags-weighted-step">23</a>. Its center terms have size $A_q(m)$. A direct estimate of $\ell_q$ would accumulate a scalar even in a filled state. We instead calibrate $K_q+\ell_q$ by a filled step at the nearest lower flux divisible by three.

Put $q^0=3\lfloor q/3\rfloor$ and $r=q-q^0\in\{0,1,2\}$. Start with the filled state at flux $q^0-3$ and increase the flux $r$ times by F transport at the same south pole. The resulting unit vector $\chi_q$ lies at flux $q-3$ and has the minimum possible hole number $r$. If $r=0$, it is the filled state itself.

<a id="stmt-lem-charge-calibration"></a>
<div id="lem:charge-calibration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:charge-calibrated-scalar"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Calibrated electron step). *For $q$ above the fixed stopping range, $$\label{eq:charge-calibrated-scalar}
 \left|\ell_q+\langle K_q\rangle_{\chi_q}\right|
 \le C\left(J_q+|\ell_q-\ell_{q^0}|
              +\frac{f(q)}{(L+q)^2}\right).$$ For any collection of northern center terms of $K_q$, their expectation in $\chi_q$ may instead be replaced by the linear reference functional of Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a> at child flux $q-3$; the additional error is at most $CJ_q$. This remains true when the choice between the two centerings is made separately on disjoint annuli.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-charge-calibration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-charge-calibration</summary>

*Proof.* In the filled E step at $q^0$, both parent and child compressions of $D(f)-C(f)\mathbf1$ vanish. Proposition <a href="#prop:flags-weighted-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flags-weighted-step">23</a> therefore gives $|\ell_{q^0}+\langle K_{q^0}\rangle|\le
C f(q^0)(L+q^0)^{-2}$. Corollary <a href="#pinning:calibration-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinning:calibration-cost">20</a> compares this filled-step expectation first with the directly embedded lower-flux state and then with its at most two F-growth transports. Its bound is $CJ_q$ for each comparison, proving <a href="#eq:charge-calibrated-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-calibrated-scalar">[eq:charge-calibrated-scalar]</a> after adding $|\ell_q-\ell_{q^0}|$.

For a northern source center, the linear reference functional of Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a>, applied at child flux $q-3$, is the expectation in the same directly embedded filled state at $q^0-3$, using the common slots. The last assertion of Corollary <a href="#pinning:calibration-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinning:calibration-cost">20</a> applies to any selected collection of source pieces and bounds the sum of absolute comparison errors by $CJ_q$. It therefore also permits the annulus-dependent choice of centering in the statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:charge-node-lower-bound"></a>
For completeness, the elementary rule for summing history bounds is as follows. At a node of flux $q$ with positive hole number, every descendant flag is at an index at most $q$, and hence $$\label{eq:charge-node-lower-bound}
 S_{L,p}\ge h f(q)\mathbf1\ge f(q)\mathbf1$$ on that node’s full descendant space. A node error bounded by $\eta_q f(q)\mathbf1$ can consequently be replaced by $\eta_q S_{L,p}$ there. If $\sum_q\eta_q\le\eta$, the sum of all these node errors is at most $\eta S_{L,p}$: on each history, each flag appears only at ancestor fluxes, each visited at most once. This is a comparison of diagonal operators after the node form bounds have been applied. It also controls an off-diagonal remainder by its operator norm. At a node with no holes, use the exact vanishing of the centered compression instead of this rule.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-charge-calibration-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-charge-calibration</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:charge-pooled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:charge-pooled">28</a> for $p=1/8$.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:flags-weighted-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flags-weighted-step">23</a> recursively to $D_q(f)-C_q(f)\mathbf1$. The norm remainder at a node is at most $C f(q)(L+q)^{-2}$, and its total cost is $o_L(1)S_{L,p}$ by <a href="#eq:charge-node-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-node-lower-bound">[eq:charge-node-lower-bound]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:charge-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-sums">30</a>. F transport has norm at most $CB_q$. Together with <a href="#eq:charge-F-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-F-scalar">[eq:charge-F-scalar]</a>, its contribution is $$-\bigl(\tfrac13-o_L(1)\bigr)f(q)$$ for the flag created at that step.

At an E step with one remaining hole, $q\equiv1\pmod3$, so $\chi_q$ also has one hole. Compare the child vector to $\chi_q$ using Corollary <a href="#cor:flags-one-hole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:flags-one-hole">27</a>. The difference of the $K_q$ expectations is at most $C\sup_m A_q(m)$. This is a two-sided form bound since it holds for every unit vector in the child space. The calibration error and all one-hole errors have total cost $o_L(1)S_{L,p}$ by Lemmas <a href="#lem:charge-scalars" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-scalars">29</a> and <a href="#lem:charge-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-sums">30</a>.

At an E step with at least two remaining holes, Proposition <a href="#pinning:one-sided" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pinning:one-sided">19</a> bounds the increase from $K_q$ by $C\int A_q(m)/w\,dm$. Its total cost is again $o_L(1)S_{L,p}$. It remains to pay the positive part of $\ell_q$. Divide that scalar equally among the remaining flags. A fixed flag then pays at most one half of each $(\ell_q)_+$ at such an ancestor. The E-step intervals are disjoint, so Lemma <a href="#lem:charge-scalars" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-scalars">29</a> bounds the total payment for a flag at $k$ by $$\left(\frac{1+p}{6(1-p)}+o_L(1)\right)f(k)
 =\bigl(\tfrac3{14}+o_L(1)\bigr)f(k).$$

At terminal fluxes there are only finitely many spaces. Uniformly on them, $f(j)=1+O(L^{-1})$, and hence $$D_q(f)-C_q(f)\mathbf1=-\frac h3\mathbf1+O(L^{-1}).$$ For $h=0$ the expression is exactly zero by rotational invariance; for $h\ge1$ the error is $O(L^{-1})h$, which is also $O(L^{-1})$ times the terminal flag weight. Terminal flags therefore receive the same credit $1/3-o_L(1)$. Combining all bounds yields $$\mathcal R_{n,q}(D_q(f)-C_q(f)\mathbf1)\mathcal R_{n,q}^*
 \le-\bigl(\tfrac13-\tfrac3{14}-o_L(1)\bigr)S_{L,p}.$$ The strict margin is $5/42$. Choose $L=L_0$ so that the total error is less than $5/84$ and take $c=5/84$. ◻

</details>

We record what this first estimate gives after rotation averaging. It will be applied to annular portions of the next transport interaction, with their center size factored out.

<a id="stmt-cor-charge-weak-average"></a>
<div id="cor:charge-weak-average" class="corollary">

**Corollary 32** (First averaged comparison). *Let $W$ be a neutral Hermitian interaction with bounded strength per unit area and fixed rapid locality bounds. For unit vectors $\psi\in Z_{n,q}$ and $\chi\in Z_{n',q}$ with hole numbers $h,h'$, <a id="eq:charge-weak-average"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-weak-average}
 |\langle W\rangle_\psi-\langle W\rangle_\chi|
 \le C_W(q+1)^{7/8}(h+h').$$ For radius-$r$ interactions of unit local norm one can take $C_W\le C(1+r)^B$ for a fixed $B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-charge-weak-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-charge-weak-average</summary>

*Proof.* To specify the rotation average on a physical zero space, let $V_{n,q}(g)$ denote its rotation representation and define $$S_{L,p}^{g}=V_{n,q}(g)\mathcal R_{n,q}^*S_{L,p}
             \mathcal R_{n,q}V_{n,q}(g)^*.$$ Thus $S_{L,p}^{g}$ acts on $Z_{n,q}$, whereas $S_{L,p}$ itself acts in history coordinates. This is the history construction obtained by rotating a fixed readout frame by $g$; its estimates are uniform in $g$. Use this convention separately in each number sector.

<a id="eq:charge-rotation-average"></a>
For a local term centered at $x(g)=g\cdot\mathrm{north}$, use Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a> in the frame rotated by $g$, with exponent $a=7/4$. The local term itself need not belong to a rotation-covariant family; the estimate is pointwise in $g$. Since $(1+b)^{-a}\le C f_{L_0,1/8}(b)$, the local comparison is bounded by the corresponding rotated $S_{L_0,1/8}$. Its reference scalar cancels between the two vectors. By irreducibility of $U_q$, normalized rotation averaging gives the one-particle identity $$\label{eq:charge-rotation-average}
 \int_{SU(2)} D_q(f)^{g}\,dg
 =\frac{\sum_{j=0}^q f(j)}{q+1}\,\mathcal N,$$ where Haar measure has mass one. Proposition <a href="#prop:charge-pooled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:charge-pooled">28</a> for $p=1/8$ consequently gives $$\int_{SU(2)}\langle S_{L_0,1/8}^{g}\rangle_\psi\,dg
 \le\frac{h}{3c(q+1)}\sum_{j=0}^q f_{L_0,1/8}(j).$$ The physical area is $2\pi q$, and the center density is bounded. Multiplying by that area and using $\sum_{j=0}^q f_{L_0,1/8}(j)\le C(q+1)^{7/8}$ proves the result. The same argument applies to any center measure with bounded mass in unit balls: spread each center uniformly over a ball of fixed radius and regard its observable as centered at the new point. The resulting measure has bounded area density, and the support radius increases by only a fixed constant. Thus the preceding integral estimate applies to both discrete and continuous center measures. Finally, a rapid shell expansion multiplies the radius-$r$ estimate by a summable series of shell norms times $(1+r)^B$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The summable weight

We now fix $p=3/2$, keeping the already chosen $L_0$ and the constant in Corollary <a href="#cor:charge-weak-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charge-weak-average">32</a> fixed. The new scale $L$ will be chosen at the end. Calibrate every E step with positive hole number by Lemma <a href="#lem:charge-calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-calibration">31</a>. Its scalar error, the branch remainders, and all F-step errors have total cost $o_L(1)S_{L,3/2}$ by the estimates already proved. It remains to bound the centered E interactions.

Divide centers into annuli $M\le1+m<2M$, where $M=1,2,4,\ldots$. Write $$a_M=\left(\frac{M}{L+M}\right)^2,\qquad f_M=f(M),\qquad
 \delta=\frac1{24}.$$ On this annulus the local interaction size is at most $C(q+1)^{-2}f_Ma_M$. Choose a fixed sufficiently large $C_0$ and separate fluxes at $T_M=C_0M^{1+\delta}$. An annulus is present only if $q+1\ge M$.

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-charge-weak-average-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-charge-weak-average</summary>

<a id="eq:charge-near-cost"></a>
<a id="eq:charge-near-sum"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:charge-pooled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:charge-pooled">28</a> for $p=3/2$.* First consider $q\le T_M$. Center the annular interaction at $\chi_q$. If the child has $h>0$ holes, the calibration state has $r\le h$ holes, so Corollary <a href="#cor:charge-weak-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charge-weak-average">32</a>, applied at child flux $q-3$, bounds the centered interaction in absolute quadratic form by $$\label{eq:charge-near-cost}
 C(q+1)^{-2}f_M a_M(q+1)^{7/8}h\mathbf1.$$ The child flag weight is at least $hf(q)$. For $M\le q+1\le T_M+1$, $f_M/f(q)\le C M^{p\delta}$. Thus summing the relative cost <a href="#eq:charge-near-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-near-cost">[eq:charge-near-cost]</a> over these fluxes gives $$\label{eq:charge-near-sum}
 C a_M M^{p\delta}\sum_{q+1\ge M}(q+1)^{-1-1/8}
 \le C a_M M^{-(1/8-p\delta)}
 =C a_M M^{-1/16}.$$ The finitely many fluxes near the stopping range obey the same estimate after increasing its constant.

<a id="eq:charge-far-sum"></a>
Now let $q>T_M$. With $C_0$ sufficiently large, the annulus lies in the common northern region. Center it by the linear reference functional in Theorem <a href="#thm:flags-local-readout" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:flags-local-readout">26</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:charge-calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:charge-calibration">31</a> accounts for this change of centering with the already budgeted error $CJ_q$. For a center in the annulus, each descendant history $\omega$ satisfies <a id="eq:charge-weight-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-weight-comparison}
 \begin{split}
 W_m(\omega)
 &\le C\sum_{k\in\mathcal B(\omega)}
          \min\left(1,\left(\frac{M}{1+k}\right)^a\right)\\
 &\le\frac C{f_M}\sum_{k\in\mathcal B(\omega)}f(k)
  =\frac C{f_M}S_{L,3/2}(\omega).
 \end{split}$$ This is also a diagonal operator inequality in the descendant history space. Indeed, for $k\le M$, $f(k)\ge f(M)$; for $k>M$, use $a=7/4>p$ and $M/(1+k)\le C(L+M)/(L+k)$. The annulus has area $O(M)$. The local readout estimate therefore bounds its centered interaction by $$C(q+1)^{-2} f_Ma_M\frac M{f_M}S_{L,3/2}.$$ For rapid interactions this formula follows by applying the radius-$r$ bound to each shell and summing its norm times $(1+r)^B$. The shell center stays in the same annulus even when its support extends beyond it; shells reaching the south are covered by the large-radius part of the readout estimate. Summing over $q>T_M$ gives $$\label{eq:charge-far-sum}
 C a_M M\sum_{q>T_M}(q+1)^{-2}S_{L,3/2}
 \le C a_M M^{-\delta}S_{L,3/2}.$$

Both resulting sums over dyadic $M$ tend to zero with $L$. Explicitly, for every $0<\eta<2$, <a id="eq:charge-dyadic-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-dyadic-sum}
 \sum_{M\in\{1,2,4,\ldots\}}
 \left(\frac M{L+M}\right)^2M^{-\eta}
 \le C_\eta L^{-\eta}.$$ For $M\le L$ this follows by summing $L^{-2}M^{2-\eta}$; for $M>L$ it follows by summing $M^{-\eta}$. Apply this with $\eta=1/16$ and $\eta=1/24$ to <a href="#eq:charge-near-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-near-sum">[eq:charge-near-sum]</a> and <a href="#eq:charge-far-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-far-sum">[eq:charge-far-sum]</a>. The node summation rule after <a href="#eq:charge-node-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-node-lower-bound">[eq:charge-node-lower-bound]</a> now gives an error $o_L(1)S_{L,3/2}$ for the entire centered E contribution.

There is no uncompensated electron scalar in this argument. Every F flag and every terminal flag contributes $-(1/3-o_L(1))f(k)$, while all E contributions and all remainders cost $o_L(1)S_{L,3/2}$. Choose a fixed $L=L_1$, after $L_0$ and the constant in Corollary <a href="#cor:charge-weak-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charge-weak-average">32</a>, so that the combined error is at most $1/6$. This proves <a href="#eq:charge-pooled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-pooled">[eq:charge-pooled]</a> with $c=1/6$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Rotation averaging and local interactions

The summability of $f_{L_1,3/2}$ removes the power of the area in Corollary <a href="#cor:charge-weak-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charge-weak-average">32</a>. We state the resulting estimate in the form needed for the energy argument.

<a id="stmt-thm-charge-local-linear"></a>
<div id="thm:charge-local-linear" class="theorem">

**Theorem 33** (Local expectations are linear in the number of holes). *Let $q=3(N-1)$ and let $W$ be a neutral Hermitian interaction whose center density and rapid locality bounds are uniform in $q$. For every unit vector $\psi\in Z_{n,q}$, <a id="eq:charge-local-linear"></a>$$\label{eq:charge-local-linear}
 \bigl|\langle W\rangle_\psi-
          \langle W\rangle_{\mathrm{Laughlin},N}\bigr|
 \le C_W(N-n).$$ For interactions supported in radius $r$ with local norm at most one, $C_W$ is bounded by $C(1+r)^B$ for a fixed exponent $B$. The same conclusion holds for zero-mode density matrices by linearity, with the expected value of $N-\mathcal N$ on the right.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-charge-local-linear"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-charge-local-linear</summary>

*Proof.* Use the local readout estimate with $a=7/4$, now comparing $W_0$ to $S_{L_1,3/2}$. Since the flux is divisible by three, the reference is the filled Laughlin expectation. The rotation average <a href="#eq:charge-rotation-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:charge-rotation-average">[eq:charge-rotation-average]</a> and the second part of Proposition <a href="#prop:charge-pooled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:charge-pooled">28</a> give $$\int_{SU(2)}\langle S_{L_1,3/2}^{g}\rangle_\psi\,dg
 \le\frac{h}{3c(q+1)}\sum_{j=0}^q f_{L_1,3/2}(j)
 \le\frac{C h}{q+1},$$ because $L_1$ is fixed and $\sum_{j\ge0}f_{L_1,3/2}(j)<\infty$. Integration over centers costs area $O(q+1)$, exactly as in the proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:charge-weak-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:charge-weak-average">32</a>. The result is $C_Wh=3C_W(N-n)$; absorb the factor three into $C_W$. Polynomial radius costs and rapid shell summability are preserved by that argument. Decomposing a density matrix into number sectors and then into pure states proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:energy"></a>
### Local removal of interaction energy

The estimate on zero modes controls the cost of a missing electron. We now connect an arbitrary state to the zero space by a local process that removes particles. Its interaction energy decays exponentially, and its expected particle loss is bounded by its initial interaction energy. In particular, adding electrons above Laughlin filling has a uniform energy cost.

<a id="eq:pair-resolution"></a>
Write $B_p=B(v_p)$, $0\le p\le 2q-2$, for the normalized pair annihilators of <a href="#eq:pair-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-rows">[eq:pair-rows]</a>, and put $d_a=2q-1$. For a rotation $g\in SU(2)$, $A(g)$ denotes the conjugate of an operator $A$ by the Fock representation. Haar measure $dg$ has mass one. The rotation average of any normalized pair row resolves the interaction: $$\label{eq:pair-resolution}
 d_a\int B_p(g)^*B_p(g)\,dg=H_q.$$ This is Schur’s lemma on the spin-$(q-1)$ pair space $V_q$.

<a id="stmt-thm-local-energy-descent"></a>
<div id="thm:local-energy-descent" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:energy-descent"></a>
**Theorem 34** (Local energy descent). *There are constants $c>0$, $L<\infty$, and $q_0$ such that, for every $q\ge q_0$, there is a measurable family of rapidly local physical operators $J_y$ on $\mathcal F_q$ satisfying $$\label{eq:energy-descent}
 \int J_y^*J_y\,d\nu_q(y)=H_q,
 \qquad
 \int J_y^*H_qJ_y\,d\nu_q(y)\le H_q^2-cH_q.$$ Every $J_y$ has a definite particle loss $r_y\in\{2,\ldots,L\}$, so that $[\mathcal N,J_y]=-r_yJ_y$. The center measure has bounded mass per unit ball, and all rapid-locality bounds are independent of $q$ and of the particle sector.*

</div>

The first operation in the construction is $B_0(g)$, which removes a pair near the point $g\cdot\mathrm{north}$. We then partially empty a fixed neighborhood of that point. This second operation suppresses the nearby pair correlations left behind by the first removal. We first identify the part of $H_q^2$ that pays for the surviving correlations.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The four-body term available for descent

<a id="eq:retained-energy"></a>
The maps $\mathcal L_k$ and $W_4$ are defined in <a href="#eq:lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift">[eq:lift]</a> and <a href="#eq:wedge-maps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wedge-maps">[eq:wedge-maps]</a>. Thus $\mathcal L_4(|u\rangle\langle v|)=B(u)^*B(v)$ and $W_4(v\otimes w)=v\wedge w$. Let $P_{q,\ell}$ be the projection in $V_q\otimes V_q$ onto total spin $2(q-1)-\ell$, for $0\le\ell\le2q-2$, and set <a id="eq:high-pair-blocks"></a>$$\label{eq:high-pair-blocks}
 R_q^{\mathrm{hi}}=\sum_{\ell=23}^{2q-2}P_{q,\ell},
 \qquad R_q^{\mathrm{lo}}=I-R_q^{\mathrm{hi}}.$$ For large $q$, $R_q^{\mathrm{lo}}$ contains precisely the small deficits $\ell=0,\ldots,22$. Proposition <a href="#prop:retained-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:retained-blocks">39</a>, proved in the appendix, gives $$\label{eq:retained-energy}
 H_q^2\ge c_0H_q+
 \mathcal L_4(W_4R_q^{\mathrm{hi}}W_4^*)
 \qquad(0<c_0<1/25)$$ for all sufficiently large $q$, uniformly on $\mathcal F_q$.

This statement contains more information than the unperturbed spectral gap. The normal-ordering identity for $H_q^2$ contains the full four-body term $\mathcal L_4(W_4W_4^*)$. The finite comparison of <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Gap](#ref-Gap), Sections 2–6] uses only the blocks $\ell\le22$. The appendix includes the finite certificate and its transfer to finite $q$, and keeps the other blocks with coefficient one. The loss construction will use precisely this part of the square.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Emptying a neighborhood without increasing distant pair energy

<a id="eq:evacuation-cutoff"></a>
Choose a smooth nondecreasing $T:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ such that $T=0$ on $(-\infty,1]$ and $T=1$ on $[3,\infty)$. We choose it flat at the two endpoints and, in addition, require $$\label{eq:evacuation-cutoff}
 |T^{(r)}(x)|\le C_r\sqrt{1-T(x)}\qquad(r\ge1).$$ For example, with $\eta(x)=e^{-1/x}$ for $x>0$ and $\eta(x)=0$ for $x\le0$, one may take $T(x)=\eta(x-1)/(\eta(x-1)+\eta(3-x))$. The estimate follows by differentiating near $x=3$; an exponential $e^{-1/(3-x)}$ times any fixed negative power of $3-x$ is bounded by a constant times $e^{-1/(2(3-x))}$. Away from that endpoint the estimate is immediate from smoothness.

For an integer $M\ge2$, put $t_j=T(j/M)$ and $n_j=c_j^*c_j$. The two operators <a id="eq:single-mode-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:single-mode-loss}
 K_{j,0}=I-n_j+t_jn_j,
 \qquad K_{j,1}=\sqrt{1-t_j^2}\,c_j$$ define a trace-preserving channel, since $K_{j,0}^*K_{j,0}+K_{j,1}^*K_{j,1}=I$. Compose these channels in increasing order of $j$, for $0\le j<3M$. Write $K_\alpha$ for the resulting products of Kraus operators, where $\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{3M}$, and write $\mathcal E_M$ for the channel. Its dual on observables is $\mathcal E_M^*(O)=\sum_\alpha K_\alpha^*OK_\alpha$.

The canonical anticommutation relations imply a useful exact formula. On an even normal-ordered monomial, the dual of the $j$th channel multiplies by $t_j$ for each occurrence of $c_j$ or $c_j^*$. Indeed, if neither occurs, the even monomial commutes with both operators of mode $j$ and completeness gives the claim. If exactly one occurs, the loss contribution vanishes and the no-loss contribution gives $t_j$. If both occur, writing them as an occupation factor gives $t_j^2$. Consequently <a id="eq:evacuated-interaction"></a>$$\label{eq:evacuated-interaction}
 \mathcal E_M^*(H_q)=\sum_{b=1}^{2q-1}\widetilde B_{b-1}^*
                                      \widetilde B_{b-1},
 \qquad
 \widetilde B_{b-1}
   =\sum_{\substack{i<j\\i+j=b}}t_it_j\,
                   \overline{v_{b-1}(i,j)}\,c_jc_i.$$ Here and below terms with an orbital index outside $\{0,\ldots,q\}$ are absent.

<a id="stmt-lem-evacuation-rows"></a>
<div id="lem:evacuation-rows" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Pair energy after local loss). *There is a sequence $\varepsilon_M\to0$ such that, for every $M\ge2$ and every $q\ge30M$, <a id="eq:evacuation-rows"></a>$$\label{eq:evacuation-rows}
 \sum_{b=1}^{2q-1}\widetilde B_{b-1}^*\widetilde B_{b-1}
 \le\sum_{b>M}B_{b-1}^*B_{b-1}+\varepsilon_MI
 \quad\hbox{on }\mathcal F_q.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-evacuation-rows"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-evacuation-rows</summary>

<a id="eq:pair-absolute-moments"></a>
<a id="eq:pair-remote-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:pair-hypergeometric"></a>
*Proof.* The following two elementary estimates specialize the general pair localization bounds to the cutoff used here. In particular, the exponential estimate on distant rows will let us preserve coefficient one on their energy. Both follow directly from the coefficients in <a href="#eq:pair-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-rows">[eq:pair-rows]</a>. For $b\le q/2$ and every fixed integer $r\ge0$, $$\label{eq:pair-absolute-moments}
 \sum_{\substack{i<j\\i+j=b}}|v_{b-1}(i,j)|\,|i-b/2|^r
 \le C_r b^{r/2+1/4}.$$ For $b>10M$ and $q\ge30M$, $$\label{eq:pair-remote-tail}
 \sum_{\substack{i<j,\ i+j=b\\i<3M}}|v_{b-1}(i,j)|
 \le Cb^{3/2}e^{-c_1b}.$$ Constants may change after decreasing $c_1>0$. Here is a direct verification. Set $$p_{q,b}(i)=\frac{\binom qi\binom q{b-i}}{\binom{2q}b}.$$ The exact pair formula gives $$\label{eq:pair-hypergeometric}
 |v_{b-1}(i,b-i)|^2
 =\frac{2(2q-1)(2i-b)^2}{b(2q-b)}p_{q,b}(i),
 \qquad
 \frac{p_{q,b}(i+1)}{p_{q,b}(i)}
 =\frac{(q-i)(b-i)}{(i+1)(q-b+i+1)}.$$ When $b\le q/2$, these ratios, their inverses, and symmetry about $b/2$ show $$p_{q,b}(i)\le Cb^{-1/2}
                  \exp\{-c_2(i-b/2)^2/b\}.$$ For completeness, on the upper half $i\ge(b-1)/2$, the first factor in the ratio is at most one and the second is at most $\exp\{-(2i+1-b)/(b+1)\}$. Summing logarithms from a middle index gives the Gaussian upper bound relative to the middle value. In the range $|i-b/2|\le\sqrt b$, the absolute value of that logarithm is at most $C(1+|i-b/2|)/b$. Summing these lower bounds and using $\sum_i p_{q,b}(i)=1$ bounds the middle value by $C/\sqrt b$. Insertion in <a href="#eq:pair-hypergeometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-hypergeometric">[eq:pair-hypergeometric]</a>, followed by summing Gaussian moments, proves <a href="#eq:pair-absolute-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-absolute-moments">[eq:pair-absolute-moments]</a>.

To prove <a href="#eq:pair-remote-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-remote-tail">[eq:pair-remote-tail]</a>, there is nothing to check if $b>q+3M$, because the sum is empty. Otherwise $b\le q+3M\le11q/10$, so $2(2q-1)/[b(2q-b)]\le C/b$ in <a href="#eq:pair-hypergeometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-hypergeometric">[eq:pair-hypergeometric]</a>. For every allowed $i\le2b/5$ and $b\ge20$, the ratio in <a href="#eq:pair-hypergeometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-hypergeometric">[eq:pair-hypergeometric]</a> is at least $4/3$. Starting with $i<3M<3b/10$ and taking at least $b/10-O(1)$ steps toward $2b/5$ gives $p_{q,b}(i)\le Ce^{-c b}$, since each probability is at most one. The square root of <a href="#eq:pair-hypergeometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-hypergeometric">[eq:pair-hypergeometric]</a> is therefore at most $C\sqrt b\,e^{-c_1b}$, and there are at most $b$ terms. This proves <a href="#eq:pair-remote-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-remote-tail">[eq:pair-remote-tail]</a>.

<a id="eq:weighted-row-error"></a>
The modified row vanishes when $b\le M$. For $M<b\le10M$, put $x=b/(2M)$ and $t=T(x)$. The smooth even function $z\mapsto T(x+z)T(x-z)$ has zero first derivative at zero. By <a href="#eq:evacuation-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:evacuation-cutoff">[eq:evacuation-cutoff]</a>, every derivative of positive order there is bounded by $C_r\sqrt{1-t}$. Taylor expansion through degree $41$, with a bounded remainder of order $42$, and <a href="#eq:pair-absolute-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-absolute-moments">[eq:pair-absolute-moments]</a> yield $$\label{eq:weighted-row-error}
 \widetilde B_{b-1}=t^2 B_{b-1}+E_b,
 \qquad
 \|E_b\|\le CM^{-3/4}\sqrt{1-t}+CM^{-20}.$$ We used $\|c_jc_i\|\le1$. More explicitly, the degree-$r$ contribution is bounded by $$C_rM^{-r}b^{r/2+1/4}\sqrt{1-t},\qquad 2\le r\le41,$$ and the remainder is at most $CM^{-42}b^{21+1/4}$.

Let $\delta=M^{-8}$ and $s=1-t+\delta$. For any vector $\phi$, Cauchy’s inequality gives $$\|(t^2B_{b-1}+E_b)\phi\|^2
 \le(t^4+s)\|B_{b-1}\phi\|^2
       +(1+t^4/s)\|E_b\phi\|^2.$$ Since $t^4+s\le1+\delta$, <a href="#eq:weighted-row-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-row-error">[eq:weighted-row-error]</a> bounds the second term by $CM^{-3/2}\|\phi\|^2$. Summing over the $O(M)$ central rows costs $CM^{-1/2}\|\phi\|^2$. The additional coefficient $\delta$ also disappears in this error: <a href="#eq:pair-absolute-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-absolute-moments">[eq:pair-absolute-moments]</a> with $r=0$ implies $\sum_{M<b\le10M}\|B_{b-1}\|^2\le CM^{3/2}$.

Finally, for $b>10M$ the coefficients of $\widetilde B_{b-1}-B_{b-1}$ are supported where $i<3M$ or $j<3M$. The increasing ordering reduces this to $i<3M$. Equation <a href="#eq:pair-remote-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-remote-tail">[eq:pair-remote-tail]</a> bounds this difference in operator norm by $Cb^{3/2}e^{-c_1b}$, while normalization of the pair vector gives $\|B_{b-1}\|\le\sqrt b$. The norm of the difference of the two row squares is therefore summable over $b>10M$, with sum at most $Ce^{-c_3M}$. Combining the three ranges proves the assertion, with $\varepsilon_M\le C(M^{-1/2}+M^{-13/2}+e^{-c_3M})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Rotation averaging suppresses the small-deficit blocks

<a id="eq:pair-tail-average"></a>
Retaining only the rows whose index sum exceeds $M$ gives a positive operator on the two pair factors: $$\label{eq:pair-tail-average}
 A_{q,M}=d_a\int
 |v_0(g)\rangle\langle v_0(g)|\otimes
 \left(\sum_{k+1>M}|v_k(g)\rangle\langle v_k(g)|\right)\,dg.$$ If the sum includes all $k$, the operator equals $I$ by Schur’s lemma. Consequently $0\le A_{q,M}\le I$. This bound handles every retained block with the same coefficient one as in <a href="#eq:retained-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retained-energy">[eq:retained-energy]</a>. It remains to make the coefficients on the finitely many unretained blocks small and bound their lifts by $H_q$.

<a id="stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail"></a>
<div id="lem:pair-spin-tail" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:spin-tail-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:fixed-block-energy"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Vanishing weight on fixed deficits). *For $A_{q,M}$ in <a href="#eq:pair-tail-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-tail-average">[eq:pair-tail-average]</a>, there are coefficients $0\le a_{q,M,\ell}\le1$ such that $$A_{q,M}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{2q-2}a_{q,M,\ell}P_{q,\ell}.$$ For every fixed $\ell\ge0$, $$\label{eq:spin-tail-limit}
 \lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{q\to\infty}a_{q,M,\ell}=0.$$ Moreover, for each fixed $\ell$ there is $C_\ell<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:fixed-block-energy}
 \mathcal L_4(W_4P_{q,\ell}W_4^*)\le C_\ell H_q$$ for all sufficiently large $q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail</summary>

*Proof.* The tensor product of two spin-$(q-1)$ representations is multiplicity free. Hence rotation invariance and $0\le A_{q,M}\le I$ prove the first assertion. Put $a=q-1$ and $d_{q,\ell}=4q-3-2\ell$. If $C_{q}(\ell,k)$ is the coupling coefficient of $v_0\otimes v_k$ in total spin $2a-\ell$ at total lowering degree $k$, then <a id="eq:spin-tail-coefficient"></a>$$\label{eq:spin-tail-coefficient}
 a_{q,M,\ell}=\frac{2q-1}{d_{q,\ell}}
                    \sum_{k+1>M}|C_q(\ell,k)|^2.$$ The unrestricted sum, including all $k$, has coefficient exactly one.

We give the fixed-$k$ limit, including its normalization. At a fixed total degree, the two spin lowering operators, divided by $\sqrt{2a}$, converge to multiplication by two independent oscillator variables $X,Y$. The highest vector of deficit $\ell$ converges to $(X-Y)^\ell/\sqrt{2^\ell\ell!}$: applying the raising operator gives successive coefficient ratio $-\sqrt{(\ell-p)/(p+1)}$ in the limit, which fixes this normalized vector. Normalized subsequent lowering multiplies by $(X+Y)/\sqrt{2(k-\ell)}$ at degree $k$. Thus the degree-$k$ coupled vector converges, in the orthonormal monomial basis, to $$\frac{(X-Y)^\ell(X+Y)^{k-\ell}}
      {2^{k/2}\sqrt{\ell!(k-\ell)!}}.$$ Its coefficient of $Y^k/\sqrt{k!}$ has squared modulus $2^{-k}\binom{k}{\ell}$, and hence $$|C_q(\ell,k)|^2\longrightarrow2^{-k}\binom{k}{\ell}.$$ There are only finitely many degrees below a fixed $M$. Subtracting these from the exact unrestricted coefficient one gives $$\lim_{q\to\infty}a_{q,M,\ell}
 =1-\frac12\sum_{\ell\le k\le M-1}2^{-k}\binom{k}{\ell}.$$ The identity $\sum_{k=\ell}^{\infty}2^{-k}\binom{k}{\ell}=2$ proves <a href="#eq:spin-tail-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spin-tail-limit">[eq:spin-tail-limit]</a>. This finite-head argument requires no uniform convergence over an unbounded set of coupling coefficients.

For <a href="#eq:fixed-block-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-block-energy">[eq:fixed-block-energy]</a>, choose a normalized highest vector $z_{q,\ell}=\sum_{p=0}^{\ell}s_pv_p\otimes v_{\ell-p}$, with $\sum_p|s_p|^2=1$. Its wedge annihilator is $\sum_p\overline{s_p}B_{\ell-p}B_p$. Every $B_{\ell-p}$ uses only the first $\ell+2$ orbitals and has norm at most $\sqrt{\ell+2}$. Therefore $$\|B(W_4z_{q,\ell})\psi\|^2
 \le(\ell+2)\sum_{p=0}^{\ell}\|B_p\psi\|^2.$$ Resolve $P_{q,\ell}$ by the orbit of its highest vector and use <a href="#eq:pair-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-resolution">[eq:pair-resolution]</a>. This yields $$\mathcal L_4(W_4P_{q,\ell}W_4^*)
 \le\frac{d_{q,\ell}}{2q-1}(\ell+2)(\ell+1)H_q,$$ whose coefficient is bounded for fixed $\ell$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pair-spin-tail</summary>

<a id="eq:loss-four-body"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:local-energy-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:local-energy-descent">34</a>.* For the moment fix $M$, and define $$J_{g,\alpha}=K_\alpha(g)B_0(g),
 \qquad d\nu_q(g,\alpha)=(2q-1)\,dg$$ with counting measure in $\alpha$. Completeness of the loss channel and <a href="#eq:pair-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-resolution">[eq:pair-resolution]</a> give $\int J_y^*J_y\,d\nu_q=H_q$. Lemma <a href="#lem:evacuation-rows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:evacuation-rows">35</a>, applied to $B_0(g)\psi$ and then averaged, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int J_y^*H_qJ_y\,d\nu_q(y)
 &=d_a\int B_0(g)^*\mathcal E_{M,g}^*(H_q)B_0(g)\,dg\notag\\
 &\le\mathcal L_4(W_4A_{q,M}W_4^*)+\varepsilon_MH_q.
 \label{eq:loss-four-body}
\end{aligned}$$ The equality between the averaged pair products and the lifted four-body operator uses ordered tensor factors. There is no factor of two: $B(v\wedge w)=B(w)B(v)$ and the lift sums each ordered pair once.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:pair-spin-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pair-spin-tail">36</a> and positivity of the lift, $$\mathcal L_4(W_4A_{q,M}W_4^*)
 \le\mathcal L_4(W_4R_q^{\mathrm{hi}}W_4^*)
       +\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{22}C_\ell a_{q,M,\ell}\right)H_q.$$ Fix $c_0=1/50$ in <a href="#eq:retained-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retained-energy">[eq:retained-energy]</a>. Choose $M$ large enough, and then $q$ large enough, that $\varepsilon_M+\sum_{\ell=0}^{22}C_\ell a_{q,M,\ell}\le c_0/2$. Equations <a href="#eq:retained-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retained-energy">[eq:retained-energy]</a> and <a href="#eq:loss-four-body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-four-body">[eq:loss-four-body]</a> prove <a href="#eq:energy-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-descent">[eq:energy-descent]</a> with $c=c_0/2$.

Each $K_\alpha$ is a product of $3M$ operators of particle loss zero or one, so $J_{g,\alpha}$ has loss between $2$ and $L=3M+2$. All modes involved in $J_{g,\alpha}$ have index below $3M$, apart from identity factors. To see operator-norm locality directly, use the frame isometry $\mathsf S_q:U_q\to\ell^2(\Lambda_q)$ and write $c(f)=B(f)$ on the slot Fock space. If $P_R$ cuts off slots outside the radius-$R$ ball around $g\cdot\mathrm{north}$, the canonical anticommutation relations give $$\|c(\mathsf S_qe_j(g))-c(P_R\mathsf S_qe_j(g))\|
   =\|(I-P_R)\mathsf S_qe_j(g)\|_2\le C_{D,M}R^{-D}.$$ The last estimate follows from Lemma <a href="#locality:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:frame">4</a> and the slot volume bound, with a larger decay order before summing squares. The same estimate for a product follows by telescoping its finitely many factors, or by Lemma <a href="#locality:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:calculus">7</a>. Thus the jumps are rapidly local around $g\cdot\mathrm{north}$, uniformly in $q$. There are only $2^{3M}$ auxiliary labels, with $M$ now fixed. The pushforward of $(2q-1)dg$ to the physical sphere has area density $(2q-1)/(2\pi q)$; the remaining rotation angle has mass one. Including all auxiliary labels therefore gives total center density $2^{3M}(2q-1)/(2\pi q)$, uniformly bounded in $q$ for this fixed $M$. These observations prove all locality and density assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Particle loss, excess charge, and a coercive Fock Hamiltonian

<a id="eq:loss-generator"></a>
Let $q=3(N-1)$ and let $\rho_t$ evolve by the trace-preserving equation whose dual generator is $$\label{eq:loss-generator}
 \mathcal D_q(O)=\int J_y^*OJ_y\,d\nu_q(y)-\tfrac12\{H_q,O\}.$$ This is a finite-dimensional Lindblad generator <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Lindblad](#ref-Lindblad)]. Positivity and trace preservation also follow directly by iterating variation of constants, with the completely positive gain map $\rho\mapsto\int J_y\rho J_y^*\,d\nu_q(y)$ and the no-jump map $\rho\mapsto e^{-tH_q/2}\rho e^{-tH_q/2}$. We write $\langle O\rangle_t=\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_tO)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-grand-canonical-coercivity"></a>
<div id="prop:grand-canonical-coercivity" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:excess-number-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:G-coercivity"></a>
<a id="eq:loss-energy-decay"></a>
<a id="eq:loss-number-control"></a>
<a id="eq:loss-hole-control"></a>
**Proposition 37** (Control of particle number). *There are constants $C_{\mathrm{num}}<\infty$, $\mu>0$, $a>0$, and $C_G<\infty$, independent of sufficiently large $q=3(N-1)$, such that $$\label{eq:excess-number-bound}
 \mathcal N-N\le C_{\mathrm{num}}H_q.$$ The operator <a id="eq:coercive-G"></a>$$\label{eq:coercive-G}
 G_q=H_q+\mu(N-\mathcal N)$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:G-coercivity}
 G_q\ge a\bigl(H_q+|N-\mathcal N|\bigr)
 \ge g\bigl(I-P_{\mathrm L,N}\bigr)$$ for a constant $g>0$, with $P_{\mathrm L,N}$ extended by zero on the other Fock sectors. For every initial density matrix, the evolution <a href="#eq:loss-generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-generator">[eq:loss-generator]</a> obeys <a id="eq:loss-G-control"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \langle H_q\rangle_t&\le e^{-ct}\langle H_q\rangle_0,
       \label{eq:loss-energy-decay}\\
 0\le\langle\mathcal N\rangle_0-\langle\mathcal N\rangle_t
    &\le C_{\mathrm{num}}\langle H_q\rangle_0,
       \label{eq:loss-number-control}\\
 \langle G_q\rangle_t&\le C_G\langle G_q\rangle_0.
       \label{eq:loss-G-control}
\end{aligned}$$ Every limit point $\rho_\infty$ is supported on $\ker H_q$, and $$\label{eq:loss-hole-control}
 0\le\operatorname{Tr}\rho_\infty(N-\mathcal N)
       \le C_G\mu^{-1}\langle G_q\rangle_0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-grand-canonical-coercivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-grand-canonical-coercivity</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:local-energy-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:local-energy-descent">34</a> gives $\mathcal D_q(H_q)\le-cH_q$, proving <a href="#eq:loss-energy-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-energy-decay">[eq:loss-energy-decay]</a>. Since $[\mathcal N,J_y]=-r_yJ_y$, $$-\mathcal D_q(\mathcal N)=\int r_yJ_y^*J_y\,d\nu_q(y),
 \qquad 2H_q\le-\mathcal D_q(\mathcal N)\le LH_q.$$ Integration proves <a href="#eq:loss-number-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-number-control">[eq:loss-number-control]</a> with $C_{\mathrm{num}}=L/c$. Compactness of the density matrices in each fixed finite-dimensional Fock space gives limit points as $t\to\infty$. By energy decay each is supported on $\ker H_q$. Lemma <a href="#lem:zero-modes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:zero-modes">2</a> shows that this kernel contains no sector of particle number exceeding $N$. Applying <a href="#eq:loss-number-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-number-control">[eq:loss-number-control]</a> to a state of definite particle number $n$ and passing to a limit point gives $n-N\le C_{\mathrm{num}}\langle H_q\rangle_0$. This proves <a href="#eq:excess-number-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:excess-number-bound">[eq:excess-number-bound]</a> on each sector, and hence on Fock space.

Choose $0<\mu\le\min(1,(2C_{\mathrm{num}})^{-1})$. In sectors $n\le N$, $G_q=H_q+\mu(N-n)$ dominates $\mu(H_q+N-n)$. In sectors $n>N$, <a href="#eq:excess-number-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:excess-number-bound">[eq:excess-number-bound]</a> gives $G_q\ge H_q/2$ and $H_q+n-N\le(1+C_{\mathrm{num}})H_q$. This proves the first inequality in <a href="#eq:G-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:G-coercivity">[eq:G-coercivity]</a> with $a=\min(\mu,[2(1+C_{\mathrm{num}})]^{-1})$. For $n\ne N$, $|N-n|\ge1$; for $n=N$, the uniform gap and uniqueness in Theorem <a href="#thm:unperturbed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:unperturbed">3</a> give $H_{N,q}\ge(1/25)(I-P_{\mathrm L,N})$. Thus the second inequality holds with $g=a/25$.

Finally, energy decay and number control give $$\langle G_q\rangle_t
 \le\langle G_q\rangle_0+\mu C_{\mathrm{num}}\langle H_q\rangle_0
 \le(1+\mu C_{\mathrm{num}}/a)\langle G_q\rangle_0.$$ Take $C_G=1+\mu C_{\mathrm{num}}/a$. Passing to a limit point, where $H_q=0$, proves <a href="#eq:loss-hole-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-hole-control">[eq:loss-hole-control]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:stability"></a>
### Relative bounds and stability

The preceding sections provide two estimates with different roles. The zero-mode bound controls a local observable after particles have been removed. The loss evolution connects an arbitrary state to that zero space at a cost measured by its initial interaction energy. We first combine them into a relative form bound. Spectral transport will then put the disorder perturbation in the class covered by that bound.

Throughout this section, $q=3(N-1)$ is sufficiently large, $\Omega\in Z_{N,q}$ is the unit Laughlin vector, and $H=H_q$. Choose the fixed $\mu>0$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:grand-canonical-coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:grand-canonical-coercivity">37</a> and write <a id="eq:stability-G"></a>$$\label{eq:stability-G}
 G=H+\mu(N-\mathcal N).$$ Thus $G$ is positive, has kernel $\mathbb C\Omega$, and has a uniform gap. Moreover it controls both $H$ and $|N-\mathcal N|$.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### A relative bound for terms that annihilate the ground state

We use the local norm $\|A\|_{x,\ell}$ of <a href="#locality:ball-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="locality:ball-norm">[locality:ball-norm]</a> at one fixed order $\ell$. Partition the sphere into cells of uniformly bounded diameter whose centers satisfy the two-dimensional volume bound of Section <a href="#sec:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:locality">3</a>. For a continuous interaction, collect all terms centered in a cell and assign their integral to that cell’s center. Moving a center a bounded distance changes the local norm by a fixed factor. We normalize the total localization norm in each cell to at most one.

Physical operators act trivially on the complementary slot modes; the inequalities below are on physical Fock space. Averaging ball approximants over total-slot-number rotations preserves support and approximation error, so approximants of a neutral operator may be chosen neutral. Taking Hermitian parts gives Hermitian approximants.

Choose $\ell$ larger than the polynomial support-radius exponent in Theorem <a href="#thm:charge-local-linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:charge-local-linear">33</a>, and larger than any fixed geometric exponents needed below. A dyadic decomposition into ball approximants shows that the zero-mode estimate extends to interactions with bounded total $\|\cdot\|_{x,\ell}$ per cell. Explicitly, the shell of radius $2^k$ has norm $O(2^{-k\ell})$, while its zero-mode cost is $O(2^{kB})$ for a fixed $B<\ell$; the series converges.

<a id="stmt-prop-relative-bound"></a>
<div id="prop:relative-bound" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:relative-bound"></a>
**Proposition 38** (Relative form bound). *For this fixed sufficiently large $\ell$, there is a constant $C$ independent of $q$ such that $$\label{eq:relative-bound}
 -CG\ \le\ \sum_x A_x\ \le\ CG$$ whenever the $A_x$ are physical, neutral, Hermitian operators satisfying $A_x\Omega=0$ and $\|A_x\|_{x,\ell}\le1$. If the sum or integral of localization norms in each cell is at most $S$, the conclusion becomes $-CSG\le\sum_xA_x\le CSG$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-relative-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-relative-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $q$ for the moment and let $K=K_q$ be the least constant in <a href="#eq:relative-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-bound">[eq:relative-bound]</a> for the stated class. It is finite: every admissible sum annihilates $\Omega$ on both sides, its norm is bounded by the finite number of cells, and $G$ has a positive gap on $\Omega^\perp$. This observation gives no useful uniform bound; we obtain one from the loss evolution.

<a id="eq:commutator-norms"></a>
Let $J_y$ be the jumps of Theorem <a href="#thm:local-energy-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:local-energy-descent">34</a>, including their bounded auxiliary indices in the measure $dy$. They obey $$\int J_y^*J_y\,dy=H,
 \qquad J_y\Omega=0\quad\text{for almost every }y.$$ The second assertion follows by taking the expectation of the first in $\Omega$. Put $C_{xy}=[A_x,J_y]$ and $D_{xy}=1+d(x,y)$. Since $A_x$ is even, the ordinary commutator vanishes for disjointly supported approximants, including when $J_y$ is odd. Lemma <a href="#locality:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:calculus">7</a>(iii) gives $$\label{eq:commutator-norms}
 \|C_{xy}\|\le C D_{xy}^{-\ell},
 \qquad \|C_{xy}\|_{x,\ell}\le C,
 \qquad
 \|C_{xy}^*C_{xy}\|_{x,\ell}\le C D_{xy}^{-\ell}.$$ The last estimate retains the same order $\ell$ required of $A_x$. Thus, after integration in $y$, the positive operators $C_{xy}^*C_{xy}$ remain in the class whose best relative-bound constant is $K$. Fixing one polynomial norm makes this closure possible.

<a id="eq:squared-commutator-sum"></a>
Every $C_{xy}$ annihilates $\Omega$, because both $A_x$ and $J_y$ do. Therefore $C_{xy}^*C_{xy}$ is a physical, positive, neutral, centered operator to which the definition of $K$ applies. Choose <a id="eq:distance-weight-choice"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-weight-choice}
 2<b<\ell-2.$$ The two-dimensional volume bound and <a href="#eq:commutator-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator-norms">[eq:commutator-norms]</a> give $$\label{eq:squared-commutator-sum}
 \sum_x\int D_{xy}^{b}C_{xy}^*C_{xy}\,dy\le C K G.$$ Indeed for each fixed $x$ the integrated operator has localization norm at most $C\int D_{xy}^{b-\ell}\,dy\le C$, and kills $\Omega$. Collect it as a single admissible term at cell $x$. This also explains the continuous-family extension in the statement.

<a id="eq:relative-derivative"></a>
Let $\rho_t$ be the state evolution dual to the loss generator, starting at an arbitrary density matrix $\rho_0$. All observables in the desired inequality preserve number, so replacing $\rho_0$ by its number-diagonal part changes none of the relevant expectations. For $A=\sum_xA_x$, the Lindblad identity reads $$\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t A)
 =\operatorname{Re}\sum_x\int
       \operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t J_y^*C_{xy})\,dy.$$ Apply Cauchy–Schwarz to this sum with weights $D_{xy}^{-b}$ and $D_{xy}^{b}$. Since $\sum_xD_{xy}^{-b}\le C$ uniformly in $y$, <a href="#eq:squared-commutator-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:squared-commutator-sum">[eq:squared-commutator-sum]</a> yields $$\label{eq:relative-derivative}
 \left|\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t A)\right|
 \le C\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t H)\bigr)^{1/2}
          \bigl(K\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t G)\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ The energy-descent estimates give constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t H)\le e^{-ct}
          \operatorname{Tr}(\rho_0 H),\qquad
 \operatorname{Tr}(\rho_t G)\le C\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_0 G),
 \qquad H\le CG.$$ Integrating <a href="#eq:relative-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-derivative">[eq:relative-derivative]</a> therefore costs at most $C\sqrt K\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_0 G)$.

Along a sequence $t_j\to\infty$, finite dimensionality gives a limit state $\rho_\infty$ supported on $Z_q$. Its sectors have $n\le N$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:zero-modes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:zero-modes">2</a>. The zero-mode bound and $\langle\Omega,A\Omega\rangle=0$ show that $$|\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_\infty A)|
 \le C\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_\infty(N-\mathcal N))
 =C\mu^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_\infty G)
 \le C\operatorname{Tr}(\rho_0G).$$ Combining the two bounds, and then taking the supremum over all admissible interactions and states, gives $$K\le C+C\sqrt K.$$ Solving this quadratic inequality for $\sqrt K$ proves a bound independent of $q$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Transporting the ground state

The perturbation is not termwise centered in the sense of Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-bound">38</a>. Spectral transport produces that property. We give the argument explicitly because it must follow the perturbed ground line rather than keep the original line fixed. The smooth frequency filter implements the quasiadiabatic idea of <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[HW](#ref-HW)] in the exact spectral-flow form of <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BMNS](#ref-BMNS)]; the locality estimates needed here were established in Section <a href="#sec:locality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:locality">3</a>.

Let $\varphi$ be real and measurable, $\|\varphi\|_\infty\le1$. On Fock space set $$V=d\Gamma(T_q(\varphi)),\qquad H_s=H+sV,
 \qquad -1\le s\le1.$$ The coherent resolution <a href="#eq:toeplitz-resolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:toeplitz-resolution">[eq:toeplitz-resolution]</a> writes $V$ as a bounded-density integral of neutral Hermitian terms $$V_x=\kappa_q\varphi(x)c(k_x)^*c(k_x).$$ They are rapidly local with uniform bounds by Lemma <a href="#locality:frame" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="locality:frame">4</a>. The same holds for $H$, using the rotations of $v_0=e_0\wedge e_1$: $$H=(2q-1)\int_{SU(2)} B(v_0(g))^*B(v_0(g))\,dg.$$ The measure has uniformly bounded density on the physical sphere. In particular every $H_s$ has uniform interaction bounds for $|s|\le1$, even though $\|V\|$ can grow with $N$.

<a id="eq:transport-filter"></a>
Fix $g_0=1/25$ and work initially on a connected parameter interval containing zero where the $N$-sector ground state is simple and the gap above it exceeds $g_0/2$. Such an interval exists at every fixed flux by finite-dimensional perturbation theory. Choose once and for all a smooth odd real function $F$ satisfying $$\label{eq:transport-filter}
 F(\omega)=-\frac1\omega\quad (|\omega|\ge g_0/2),
 \qquad F(\omega)=0\quad (|\omega|\le g_0/4).$$ It can be chosen with an inverse Fourier kernel having every absolute polynomial moment. The $1/\omega$ tail is square-integrable and has integrable derivatives of all positive orders; near time zero Plancherel gives local integrability, and repeated integration by parts gives arbitrary decay for large time.

For each term of $V$, define $Y_x(s)$ by applying this frequency filter with respect to $H_s$. In an eigenbasis of $H_s$, <a id="eq:transport-matrix-elements"></a>$$\label{eq:transport-matrix-elements}
 \langle a|Y_x(s)|b\rangle
 =F(E_a-E_b)\langle a|V_x|b\rangle.$$ Oddness of $F$ makes $Y_x$ anti-Hermitian. Every $Y_x$ is physical, neutral, and rapidly local with uniform bounds; put $Y(s)=\int Y_x(s)\,dA(x)$ and solve $$U'(s)=Y(s)U(s),\qquad U(0)=I.$$ This unitary preserves number. If $P_s$ is the $N$-sector ground projection, the usual spectral derivative formula and <a href="#eq:transport-filter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-filter">[eq:transport-filter]</a> give $P_s'=[Y(s),P_s]$. Consequently $U(s)P_0U(s)^*=P_s$. Neutrality is important here: the matrix elements crossing the ground projection stay in the $N$-particle sector. No assertion about a perturbed Fock-space gap is needed.

For each $x$ separately, define <a id="eq:transport-centered-terms"></a>$$\label{eq:transport-centered-terms}
 \begin{split}
 Q_x(s)&=U(s)^*\bigl(V_x+[H_s,Y_x(s)]\bigr)U(s),\\
 a_x(s)&=\langle\Omega,Q_x(s)\Omega\rangle,
 \qquad A_x(s)=Q_x(s)-a_x(s)I.
 \end{split}$$ These are Hermitian neutral physical operators. For an excited $N$-sector eigenvector $a$ and the ground vector $0$, their unconjugated off-diagonal matrix element is $$\bigl(1+(E_a-E_0)F(E_a-E_0)\bigr)
       \langle a|V_x|0\rangle=0.$$ Hermiticity gives the reverse vanishing. Thus each $Q_x(s)$ preserves $\mathbb C\Omega$, and $A_x(s)\Omega=0$ exactly. The commutator with $H_s$ and the finite-parameter conjugation by $U(s)$ preserve rapid locality, uniformly on the bootstrap interval. The scalar satisfies $|a_x(s)|\le\|Q_x(s)\|$; it can be assigned to the same center. Hence the interaction $A_x(s)$ satisfies Proposition <a href="#prop:relative-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:relative-bound">38</a> with a uniform size bound.

<a id="eq:final-form-bound"></a>
Let $E_0(s)$ be the $N$-sector ground energy. Differentiating the pulled-back Hamiltonian and taking its expectation in $\Omega$ gives <a id="eq:transport-relative-derivative"></a>$$\label{eq:transport-relative-derivative}
 \frac{d}{ds}\bigl(U(s)^*H_sU(s)-E_0(s)I\bigr)
 =\int A_x(s)\,dA(x),
 \qquad
